Chapter 12
Appendix

12.1
Appendix of
Chapter

3

12.1.1
Lemmas

We derive here some lemmas which will be useful for the proofs.

Lemma 12.1

(Lemma 6 in
(
Paty and Cuturi 2019
)
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{N}
be two Riemannian manifolds. Let
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
be a measurable map and
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M})
. Then,

\Pi(f_{\#}\mu,f_{\#}\nu)=\{(f\otimes f)_{\#}\gamma,\ \gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)\}.

(12.1)

Proof.

This is a straightforward extension of
(
Paty and Cuturi 2019
, Lemma 6)
.
∎

Lemma 12.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},g)
be a Hadamard manifold with origin
o
. Let
v\in T_{o}\mathcal{M}
, then

1.

the geodesic projection
P^{v}
is 1-Lipschitz.

2.

the Busemann function
B^{v}
is 1-Lipschitz.

Proof.

1.

By
Proposition

3.1
, we know that

\forall x,y\in\mathcal{M},\ |P^{v}(x)-P^{v}(y)|=d\big(\tilde{P}^{v}(x),\tilde{P}^{v}(y)\big).

(12.2)

Moreover, by
(
Ballmann et al. 2006
, Page 9)
,
\tilde{P}^{v}
is 1-Lipschitz, so is
P^{v}
.

2.

The Busemann function is 1-Lipschitz, see
e.g.

(
Bridson and Haefliger 2013
, II. Proposition 8.22)
.

∎

Lemma 12.3
.

Let
d
be a metric on
\mathcal{M}
. Then, for any
p\geq 1
,

\forall x,y\in\mathcal{M},\ d(x,y)^{p}\leq 2^{p-1}\big(d(x,o)^{p}+d(o,y)^{p}\big).

(12.3)

Lemma 12.4

(Lemma 1 in
(
Rakotomamonjy et al. 2021
)
adapted from Theorem 2 in
(
Fournier and Guillin 2015
)
)
.

Let
p\geq 1
and
\eta\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R})
. Denote
\tilde{M}_{q}(\eta)=\int|x|^{q}\ \mathrm{d}\eta(x)
the moments of order
q
and assume that
M_{q}(\eta)<\infty
for some
q>p
. Then, there exists a constant
C_{p,q}
depending only on
p,q
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}[W_{p}^{p}(\hat{\eta}_{n},\eta)]\leq C_{p,q}\tilde{M}_{q}(\eta)^{p/q}\left(n^{-1/2}\mathbb{1}_{\{q>2p\}}+n^{-1/2}\log(n)\mathbb{1}_{\{q=2p\}}+n^{-(q-p)/q}\mathbb{1}_{\{q\in(p,2p)\}}\right).

(12.4)

Lemma 12.5
.

Let
y\in\mathcal{M}
and denote for all
x\in\mathcal{M}
,
f(x)=d(x,y)^{2}
. Then,
\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}f(x)=-2\log_{x}(y)
.

For references about
Lemma

12.5
, see
e.g.

(
Chewi et al. 2020
, Appendix A)
or
(
Goto and Sato 2021
)
.

12.1.2
Proofs of
Section

3.3

Proof of
Proposition

3.1

Proof of
Proposition

3.1
.

Let
x,y\in\mathcal{G}^{v}
. Then, there exists
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
such that
x=\exp_{o}(sv)
and
y=\exp_{o}(tv)
. By a simple calculation, we have on one hand that

\displaystyle\mathrm{sign}(\langle\log_{o}(x),v\rangle_{o})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}(\langle\log_{o}(\exp_{o}(sv)),v\rangle_{o})

(12.5)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}(s\|v\|_{o}^{2})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}(s),

using that
\log_{o}\circ\exp_{o}=\mathrm{Id}
. And similarly,
\mathrm{sign}(\langle\log_{o}(y),v\rangle_{o})=\mathrm{sign}(t)
.

Then, by noting that
o=\exp_{o}(0)
, and recalling that
d(x,y)=d(\exp_{o}(tv),\exp_{o}(sv))=|t-s|
,

\displaystyle|t^{v}(x)-t^{v}(y)|

\displaystyle=|\mathrm{sign}(\langle\log_{o}(x),v\rangle_{o})d(x,o)-\mathrm{sign}(\langle\log_{o}(y),v\rangle_{o}d(y,o)|

(12.6)

\displaystyle=\big|\mathrm{sign}(s)d(\exp_{o}(sv),\exp_{o}(0))-\mathrm{sign}(t)d(\exp_{o}(tv),\exp_{o}(0))\big|

\displaystyle=\big|\mathrm{sign}(s)|s|-\mathrm{sign}(t)|t|\big|

\displaystyle=|s-t|

\displaystyle=d(x,y).

∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.2

Proof of
Proposition

3.2
.

We want to solve:

P^{v}(x)=\argmin_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\ d\big(\gamma(t),x\big)^{2},

(12.7)

where
\gamma(t)=\exp_{o}(tv)
.
For
t\in\mathbb{R}
, let
g(t)=d\big(\gamma(t),x\big)^{2}=f\big(\gamma(t)\big)
where
f(x)=d(x,y)^{2}
for
x,y\in\mathcal{M}
. Then, by
Lemma

12.5
, we have for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(t)=0

\displaystyle\iff\langle\gamma^{\prime}(t),\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}f\big(\gamma(t)\big)\rangle_{\gamma(t)}=0

(12.8)

\displaystyle\iff\langle\gamma^{\prime}(t),-2\log_{\gamma(t)}(x)\rangle_{\gamma(t)}=0.

∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.3

Proof of
Proposition

3.3
.

First, we note that
P^{v}=t^{v}\circ\tilde{P}^{v}
. Then, by using
Lemma

12.1
which states that
\Pi(f_{\#}\mu,f_{\#}\nu)=\{(f\otimes f)_{\#}\gamma,\ \gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)\}
for any
f
measurable, as well as that by
Proposition

3.1
,
|t^{v}(x)-t^{v}(y)|=d(x,y)
, we have:

\displaystyle W_{p}^{p}(P^{v}_{\#}\mu,P^{v}_{\#}\nu)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(P^{v}_{\#}\mu,P^{v}_{\#}\nu)}\ \int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}|x-y|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

(12.9)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}|x-y|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}(P^{v}\otimes P^{v})_{\#}\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int_{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}\ |P^{v}(x)-P^{v}(y)|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int_{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}|t^{v}(\tilde{P}^{v}(x))-t^{v}(\tilde{P}^{v}(y))|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int_{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}d\big(\tilde{P}^{v}(x),\tilde{P}^{v}(y)\big)^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int_{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}\ d(x,y)^{p}\ \mathrm{d}(\tilde{P}^{v}\otimes\tilde{P}^{v})_{\#}\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\tilde{P}^{v}_{\#}\mu,\tilde{P}^{v}_{\#}\nu)}\ \int_{\mathcal{G}^{v}\times\mathcal{G}^{v}}d(x,y)^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle=W_{p}^{p}(\tilde{P}^{v}_{\#}\mu,\tilde{P}^{v}_{\#}\nu).

∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.4

Proof of
Proposition

3.4
.

First, let us compute
t^{v}\circ\tilde{B}^{v}
:

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{M},\ t^{v}(\tilde{B}^{v}(x))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}(\langle\log_{o}(\tilde{B}^{v}(x)),v\rangle_{o})d(\tilde{B}^{v}(x),o)

(12.10)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}(-B^{\gamma}(x)\|v\|_{o}^{2})d(\exp_{o}(-B^{v}(x)v),\exp_{o}(0))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}(-B^{\gamma}(x))|-B^{v}(x)|

\displaystyle=-B^{v}(x).

Then, using the same computation as in the proof of
Proposition

3.3
, we get

W_{p}^{p}(B^{v}_{\#}\mu,B^{v}_{\#}\nu)=W_{p}^{p}(\tilde{B}^{v}_{\#}\mu,\tilde{B}^{v}_{\#}\nu).

(12.11)

∎

12.1.3
Proofs of
Section

3.4

Proof of
Proposition

3.5

Proof of
Proposition

3.5
.

First, we will show that for any
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{M})
,
\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)<\infty
. Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{M})
, and let
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
be an arbitrary coupling between them.
Then by using first
Lemma

12.1
followed by the 1-Lipschitzness of the projections
Lemma

12.2
and
Lemma

12.3
, we obtain

\displaystyle W_{p}^{p}(P^{v}_{\#}\mu,P^{v}_{\#}\nu)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int|P^{v}(x)-P^{v}(y)|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

(12.12)

\displaystyle\leq\int|P^{v}(x)-P^{v}(y)|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\int d(x,y)^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\left(\int d(x,o)^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\int d(o,y)^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\nu(y)\right)

\displaystyle<\infty.

Hence, we can conclude that
\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)<\infty
.

Now, let us show that it is a pseudo-distance. First, it is straightforward to see that
\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)\geq 0
, that it is symmetric,
i.e.

\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)=\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\nu,\mu)
, and that
\mu=\nu
implies that
\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)=0
using that
W_{p}
is well a distance.

For the triangular inequality, we can derive it using the triangular inequality for
W_{p}
and the Minkowski inequality. Let
\mu,\nu,\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{M})
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{S_{o}}W_{p}^{p}(P^{v}_{\#}\mu,P^{v}_{\#}\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

(12.13)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{S_{o}}\big(W_{p}(P^{v}_{\#}\mu,P^{v}_{\#}\alpha)+W_{p}(P^{v}_{\#}\alpha,P^{v}_{\#}\nu)\big)^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{S_{o}}W_{p}^{p}(P^{v}_{\#}\mu,P^{v}_{\#}\alpha)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+\left(\int_{S_{o}}W_{p}^{p}(P^{v}_{\#}\alpha,P^{v}_{\#}\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\mu,\alpha)+\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\alpha,\nu).

∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.6

Proof of
Proposition

3.6
.

Let
f\in L^{1}(\mathcal{M})
,
g\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}\times S_{o})
, then by Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{CHR}f,g\rangle_{\mathbb{R}\times S_{o}}

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{o}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{CHR}f(t,v)g(t,v)\ \mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\lambda(v)

(12.14)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{o}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}f(x)\mathbb{1}_{\{t=P^{v}(x)\}}g(t,v)\ \mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(x)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\lambda(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}f(x)\int_{S_{o}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(t,v)\mathbb{1}_{\{t=P^{v}(x)\}}\ \mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\lambda(v)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}f(x)\int_{S_{o}}g\big(P^{v}(x),v\big)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}f(x)\mathrm{CHR}^{*}g(x)\ \mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(x)

\displaystyle=\langle f,\mathrm{CHR}^{*}g\rangle_{\mathcal{M}}.

∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.7

Proof of
Proposition

3.7
.

We follow the proof of
(
Boman and Lindskog 2009
, Lemma 1)
. On one hand,
g\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}\times S_{o})
, thus for all
\epsilon>0
, there exists
M>0
such that
|t|\geq M
implies
|g(t,v)|\leq\epsilon
for all
v\in S_{o}
.

Let
\epsilon>0
and
M>0
which satisfies the previous property. Denote
E(x,M)=\{v\in S_{o},\ |P^{v}(x)|<M\}
. Then, as
d(x,o)>0
, we have

E(x,M)=\{v\in S_{o},\ |P^{v}(x)|<M\}=\left\{v\in S_{p},\ \frac{P^{v}(x)}{d(x,o)}<\frac{M}{d(x,o)}\right\}\xrightarrow[d(x,o)\to\infty]{}\emptyset.

(12.15)

Thus,
\lambda\big(E(x,M)\big)\xrightarrow[d(x,o)\to\infty]{}0
. Choose
M^{\prime}
such that
d(x,o)>M^{\prime}
implies that
\lambda\big(E(x,M)\big)<\epsilon
.

Then, for
x\in\mathcal{M}
such that
|P^{v}(x)|\geq\max(M,M^{\prime})
(and thus
d(x,o)\geq M^{\prime}
since
|P^{v}(x)\leq d(x,o)
as
P^{v}
is Lipschitz,

\displaystyle|\mathrm{CHR}^{*}g(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int_{E(x,M)}g(P^{v}(x),v)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)\right|+\left|\int_{E(x,M)^{c}}g(P^{v}(x),v)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)\right|

(12.16)

\displaystyle\leq\|g\|_{\infty}\ \lambda\big(E(x,M)\big)+\epsilon\lambda\big(E(x,M)^{c}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\|g\|_{\infty}\epsilon+\epsilon.

Thus, we showed that
\mathrm{CHR}^{*}g(x)\xrightarrow[d(x,o)\to\infty]{}0
, and thus
\mathrm{CHR}^{*}g\in C_{0}(\mathcal{M})
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.8

Proof of
Proposition

3.8
.

Let
g\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}\times S_{o})
, as
\mathrm{CHR}\mu=\lambda\otimes K
, we have by definition

\int_{S_{o}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(t,v)\ K(v,\cdot)_{\#}\mu(\mathrm{d}t)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times S_{o}}g(t,v)\ \mathrm{d}(\mathrm{CHR}\mu)(t,v).

(12.17)

Hence, using the property of the dual, we have for all
g\in C_{o}(\mathbb{R}\times S_{o})
,

\displaystyle\int_{S_{o}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(t,v)\ K(v,\cdot)_{\#}\mu(\mathrm{d}t)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times S_{o}}g(t,v)\ \mathrm{d}(\mathrm{CHR}\mu)(t,v)

(12.18)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\mathrm{CHR}^{*}g(x)\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\int_{S_{o}}g(P^{v}(x),v)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{o}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}g(P^{v}(x),v)\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\lambda(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{o}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(t,v)\ \mathrm{d}(P^{v}_{\#}\mu)(t)\mathrm{d}\lambda(v).

Hence, for
\lambda
-almost every
v\in S_{o}
,
K(v,\cdot)_{\#}\mu=P^{v}_{\#}\mu
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.9

Proof of
Proposition

3.9
.

Using
Lemma

12.1
and that the projections are 1-Lipschitz (
Lemma

12.2
), we can show that, for any
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{M})
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int|P^{v}(x)-P^{v}(y)|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y).

(12.19)

Let
\gamma^{*}\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
being an optimal coupling for the Wasserstein distance with ground cost
d
, then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\int|P^{v}(x)-P^{v}(y)|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma^{*}(x,y)

(12.20)

\displaystyle\leq\int d(x,y)^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma^{*}(x,y)

\displaystyle=W_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu).

∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.10

Proof of
Proposition

3.10
.

This proof follows the proof in the Euclidean case derived in
(
Bonnotte 2013
, Proposition 5.1.7)
or in
(
Candau-Tilh 2020
, Proposition 1.33)
.

As
\mu
is absolutely continuous,
P^{v}_{\#}\mu
is also absolutely continuous and there is a Kantorovitch potential
\psi_{v}
between
P^{v}_{\#}\mu
and
P^{v}_{\#}\nu
. Moreover, as the support is restricted to a compact, it is Lipschitz and thus differentiable almost everywhere.

First, using the duality formula, we obtain the following lower bound for all
\epsilon>0
,

\frac{\mathrm{CHSW}_{2}^{2}\big((T_{\epsilon})_{\#}\mu,\nu\big)-\mathrm{CHSW}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)}{2\epsilon}\geq\int_{S_{o}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\frac{\psi_{v}(P^{v}(T_{\epsilon}(x)))-\psi_{v}(P^{v}(x))}{\epsilon}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\lambda(v).

(12.21)

Then, we know that the exponential map satisfies
\exp_{x}(0)=x
and
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\exp(tv)|_{t=0}=v
. Taking the limit
\epsilon\to 0
, the right term is equal to
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}g(t)|_{t=0}
with
g(t)=\psi_{v}(P^{v}(T_{t}(x)))
and is equal to

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}g(t)|_{t=0}=\psi_{v}^{\prime}(P^{v}(T_{0}(x)))\langle\nabla P^{v}(T_{0}(x)),\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}T_{t}(x)|_{t=0}\rangle_{x}=\psi_{v}^{\prime}(P^{v}(x))\langle\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}P^{v}(x),\xi(x)\rangle_{x}.

(12.22)

Therefore, by the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem (we have the convergence
\lambda
-almost surely and
|\psi_{v}(P^{v}(T_{\epsilon}(x)))-\psi_{v}(P^{v}(x))|\leq\epsilon
using that
\psi_{v}
and
P^{v}
are Lipschitz and that
d\big(\exp_{x}(\epsilon\xi(x)),\exp_{x}(0)\big)\leq C\epsilon
),

\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\ \frac{\mathrm{CHSW}_{2}^{2}\big((T_{\epsilon})_{\#}\mu,\nu\big)-\mathrm{CHSW}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)}{2\epsilon}\geq\int_{S_{o}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\psi_{v}^{\prime}(P^{v}(x))\langle\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}P^{v}(x),\xi(x)\rangle\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\lambda(v).

(12.23)

For the upper bound, first, let
\pi^{v}\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
an optimal coupling. Then by
Lemma

12.1
,
\tilde{\pi}^{v}=(P^{v}\otimes P^{v})_{\#}\pi^{v}\in\Pi(P^{v}_{\#}\mu,P^{v}_{\#}\nu)
is an optimal coupling for the regular quadratic cost and for
\tilde{\pi}^{v}
-almost every
(x,y)
,
y=x-\psi_{v}^{\prime}(x)
and thus for
\pi^{v}
-almost every
(x,y)
,
P^{v}(y)=P^{v}(x)-\psi_{v}^{\prime}\big(P^{v}(x)\big)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{CHSW}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{o}}W_{2}^{2}(P^{v}_{\#}\mu,P^{v}_{\#}\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)

(12.24)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{o}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}|x-y|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\pi}^{v}(x,y)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{o}}\int_{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}}|P^{v}(x)-P^{v}(y)|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\pi(x,y)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v).

On the other hand,
((P^{v}\circ T_{\epsilon})\otimes P^{v})_{\#}\pi^{v}\in\Pi(P^{v}_{\#}(T_{\epsilon})_{\#}\mu,P^{v}_{\#}\nu)
and hence

\displaystyle\mathrm{CHSW}_{2}^{2}\big((T_{\epsilon})_{\#}\mu,\nu\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{o}}W_{2}^{2}(P^{v}_{\#}(T_{\epsilon})_{\#}\mu,P^{v}_{\#}\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)

(12.25)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{S_{o}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}|P^{v}(T_{\epsilon}(x))-P^{v}(y)|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\pi^{v}(x,y)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{CHSW}_{2}^{2}\big((T_{\epsilon})_{\#}\mu,\nu\big)-\mathrm{CHSW}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)}{2\epsilon}\leq\int_{S_{o}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}\frac{|P^{v}(T_{\epsilon}(x))-P^{v}(y)|^{2}-|P^{v}(x)-P^{v}(y)|^{2}}{2\epsilon}\ \mathrm{d}\pi^{v}(x,y)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v).

(12.26)

Note
g(\epsilon)=\big(P^{v}(T_{\epsilon}(x))-P^{v}(y)\big)^{2}
. Then,
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\epsilon}g(\epsilon)|_{\epsilon=0}=2\big(P^{v}(x)-P^{v}(y)\big)\langle\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}P^{v}(x),\xi(x)\rangle_{x}
. But, as for
\pi^{v}
-almost every
(x,y)
,
P^{v}(y)=P^{v}(x)-\psi_{v}^{\prime}(P^{v}(x))
, we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\epsilon}g(\epsilon)|_{\epsilon=0}=2\psi_{v}^{\prime}\big(P^{v}(x)\big)\langle\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}P^{v}(x),\xi(x)\rangle_{x}.

(12.27)

Finally, by the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem, we obtain

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mathrm{CHSW}_{2}^{2}\big((T_{\epsilon})_{\#}\mu,\nu\big)-\mathrm{CHSW}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)}{2\epsilon}\leq\int_{S_{o}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\psi_{v}^{\prime}(P^{v}(x))\langle\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}P^{v}(x),\xi(x)\rangle_{x}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\lambda(v).

(12.28)

∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.11

Proof of
Proposition

3.11
.

Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{M})
, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{o}}W_{p}^{p}(P^{v}_{\#}\mu,P^{v}_{\#}\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)

(12.29)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{o}}\|F_{P^{v}_{\#}\mu}^{-1}-F_{P^{v}_{\#}\nu}^{-1}\|^{p}_{L^{p}([0,1])}\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{o}}\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{P^{v}_{\#}\mu}^{-1}(q)-F_{P^{v}_{\#}\nu}^{-1}(q)\big)^{p}\ \mathrm{d}q\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)

\displaystyle=\|\Phi(\mu)-\Phi(\nu)\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{p}.

Thus,
\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}
is Hilbertian.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.13

Proof of
Proposition

3.13
.

First, using the triangular inequality, the reverse triangular inequality and the Jensen inequality for
x\mapsto x^{1/p}
(which is concave since
p\geq 1
), we have the following inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)|]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[|\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\nu)+\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\nu)-\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)|]

(12.30)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[|\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\nu)|]+\mathbb{E}[|\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\nu)-\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)|]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\nu,\hat{\nu}_{n})]+\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\mu,\hat{\mu}_{n})]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}^{p}(\nu,\hat{\nu}_{n})]^{1/p}+\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\hat{\mu}_{n})]^{1/p}.

Moreover, by Fubini-Tonelli,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}^{p}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{S_{o}}W_{p}^{p}(P^{v}_{\#}\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v)\right]

(12.31)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{o}}\mathbb{E}[W_{p}^{p}(P^{v}_{\#}\hat{\mu}_{n},P^{v}_{\#}\mu)]\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v).

Then, by applying
Lemma

12.4
, we get that for
q>p
, there exists a constant
C_{p,q}
such that,

\mathbb{E}[W_{p}^{p}(P^{v}_{\#}\hat{\mu}_{n},P^{v}_{\#}\nu)]\leq C_{p,q}\tilde{M}_{q}(P^{v}_{\#}\mu)^{p/q}\left(n^{-1/2}\mathbb{1}_{\{q>2p\}}+n^{-1/2}\log(n)\mathbb{1}_{\{q=2p\}}+n^{-(q-p)/q}\mathbb{1}_{\{q\in(p,2p)\}}\right).

(12.32)

Then, noting that necessarily,
P^{v}(o)=0
(for both the horospherical and geodesic projection, since the geodesic is of the form
\exp_{o}(tv)
), and using that
P^{v}
is 1-Lipschitz
Lemma

12.2
, we can bound the moments as

\displaystyle\tilde{M}_{q}(P^{v}_{\#}\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x|^{q}\ \mathrm{d}(P^{v}_{\#}\mu)(x)

(12.33)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}|P^{v}(x)|^{q}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}|P^{v}(x)-P^{v}(o)|^{q}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{M}}d(x,o)^{q}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=M_{q}(\mu).

Therefore, we have

\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}^{p}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu)]\leq C_{p,q}M_{q}(\mu)^{p/q}\left(n^{-1/2}\mathbb{1}_{\{q>2p\}}+n^{-1/2}\log(n)\mathbb{1}_{\{q=2p\}}+n^{-(q-p)/q}\mathbb{1}_{\{q\in(p,2p)\}}\right),

(12.34)

and similarly,

\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}^{p}(\hat{\nu}_{n},\nu)]\leq C_{p,q}M_{q}(\nu)^{p/q}\left(n^{-1/2}\mathbb{1}_{\{q>2p\}}+n^{-1/2}\log(n)\mathbb{1}_{\{q=2p\}}+n^{-(q-p)/q}\mathbb{1}_{\{q\in(p,2p)\}}\right).

(12.35)

Hence, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}[|\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)|]\leq 2C_{p,q}^{1/p}M_{q}(\nu)^{1/q}\begin{cases}n^{-1/(2p)}\ \text{if }q>2p\\
n^{-1/(2p)}\log(n)^{1/p}\ \text{if }q=2p\\
n^{-(q-p)/(pq)}\ \text{if }q\in(p,2p).\end{cases}

(12.36)

∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.14

Proof of
Proposition

3.14
.

Let
(v_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{L}
be iid samples of
\lambda
. Then, by first using Jensen inequality and then remembering that
\mathbb{E}_{v}[W_{p}^{p}(P^{v}_{\#}\mu,P^{v}_{\#}\nu)]=\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{v}\left[|\widehat{\mathrm{CHSW}}_{p,L}^{p}(\mu,\nu)-\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)|\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{v}\left[\left|\widehat{\mathrm{CHSW}}_{p,L}^{p}(\mu,\nu)-\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)\right|^{2}\right]

(12.37)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{v}\left[\left|\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\big(W_{p}^{p}(P^{v_{\ell}}_{\#}\mu,P^{v_{\ell}}_{\#}\nu)-\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)\big)\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L^{2}}\mathrm{Var}_{v}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}W_{p}^{p}(P^{v_{\ell}}_{\#}\mu,P^{v_{\ell}}_{\#}\nu)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\mathrm{Var}_{v}\left(W_{p}^{p}(P^{v}_{\#}\mu,P^{v}_{\#}\nu)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\int_{S_{o}}\left(W_{p}^{p}(P^{v}_{\#}\mu,P^{v}_{\#}\nu)-\mathrm{CHSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)\right)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(v).

∎

12.2
Appendix of
Chapter

4

12.2.1
Proofs of
Section

4.3

Proof of
Proposition

4.1

Proof of
Proposition

4.1
.

1.

Lorentz model.
Any point
y
on the geodesic obtained by the intersection between
E=\mathrm{span}(x^{0},v)
and
\mathbb{L}^{d}
can be written as

y=\cosh(t)x^{0}+\sinh(t)v,

(12.38)

where
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, as
\arccosh
is an increasing function, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{P}^{v}(x)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{y\in E\cap\mathbb{L}^{d}}\ d_{\mathbb{L}}(x,y)

(12.39)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{y\in E\cap\mathbb{L}^{d}}\ -\langle x,y\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}.

This problem is equivalent with solving

\argmin_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\ -\cosh(t)\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}-\sinh(t)\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}.

(12.40)

Let
g(t)=-\cosh(t)\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}-\sinh(t)\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}
, then

g^{\prime}(t)=0\iff\tanh(t)=-\frac{\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}{\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}.

(12.41)

Finally, using that
1-\tanh^{2}(t)=\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}(t)}
and
\cosh^{2}(t)-\sinh^{2}(t)=1
, and observing that necessarily,
\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}\leq 0
, we obtain

\cosh(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\left(-\frac{\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}{\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}\right)^{2}}}=\frac{-\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}{\sqrt{\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}-\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}},

(12.42)

and

\sinh(t)=\frac{-\frac{\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}{\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}}{\sqrt{1-\left(-\frac{\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}{\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}\right)^{2}}}=\frac{\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}{\sqrt{\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}-\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}}.

(12.43)

2.

Poincaré ball.
A geodesic passing through the origin on the Poincaré ball is of the form
\gamma(t)=tp
for an ideal point
p\in S^{d-1}
and
t\in]-1,1[
. Using that
\arccosh
is an increasing function, we find

\displaystyle\tilde{P}^{p}(x)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{y\in\mathrm{span}(\gamma)}\ d_{\mathbb{B}}(x,y)

(12.44)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{tp}\ \arccosh\left(1+2\frac{\|x-\gamma(t)\|_{2}^{2}}{(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})(1-\|\gamma(t)\|_{2}^{2})}\right)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{tp}\ \log\big(\|x-\gamma(t)\|_{2}^{2}\big)-\log\big(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}\big)-\log\big(1-\|\gamma(t)\|_{2}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{tp}\ \log\big(\|x-tp\|_{2}^{2}\big)-\log\big(1-t^{2}\big).

Let
g(t)=\log\big(\|x-tp\|_{2}^{2}\big)-\log\big(1-t^{2}\big)
. Then,

g^{\prime}(t)=0\iff\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}t^{2}-\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{\langle x,p\rangle}t+1=0&\mbox{ if }\langle p,x\rangle\neq 0,\\
t=0&\mbox{ if }\langle p,x\rangle=0.\end{array}\right.

(12.45)

Finally, if
\langle p,x\rangle\neq 0
, the solution is

t=\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}\pm\sqrt{\left(\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}\right)^{2}-1}.

(12.46)

Now, let us suppose that
\langle x,p\rangle>0
. Then,

\displaystyle\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}+\sqrt{\left(\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}\right)^{2}-1}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}

(12.47)

\displaystyle\geq 1,

because
\|x-p\|_{2}^{2}\geq 0
implies that
\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}\geq 1
, and therefore the solution is

t=\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}-\sqrt{\left(\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}\right)^{2}-1}.

(12.48)

Similarly, if
\langle x,p\rangle<0
, then

\displaystyle\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}-\sqrt{\left(\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}\right)^{2}-1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}

(12.49)

\displaystyle\leq-1,

because
\|x+p\|_{2}^{2}\geq 0
implies
\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}\leq-1
, and the solution is

\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}+\sqrt{\left(\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}\right)^{2}-1}.

(12.50)

Thus,

\displaystyle s(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}-\sqrt{\left(\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}\right)^{2}-1}\quad\text{if }\langle x,p\rangle>0\\
\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}+\sqrt{\left(\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}\right)^{2}-1}\quad\text{if }\langle x,p\rangle<0.\end{cases}

(12.51)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}-\mathrm{sign}(\langle x,p\rangle)\sqrt{\left(\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}\right)^{2}-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}-\frac{\mathrm{sign}(\langle x,p\rangle)}{2\mathrm{sign}(\langle x,p\rangle)\langle x,p\rangle}\sqrt{(1+\|x\|_{2}^{2})^{2}-4\langle x,p\rangle^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\|x\|_{2}^{2}-\sqrt{(1+\|x\|_{2}^{2})^{2}-4\langle x,p\rangle^{2}}}{2\langle x,p\rangle}.

∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.2

Proof of
Proposition

4.2
.

1.

Lorentz model.
The coordinate on the geodesic can be obtained as

P^{v}(x)=\argmin_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\ d_{\mathbb{L}}\big(\exp_{x^{0}}(tv),x\big).

(12.52)

Hence, by using (
12.41
), we obtain that the optimal
t
satisfies

\tanh(t)=-\frac{\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}{\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}\iff t=\arctanh\left(-\frac{\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}{\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}\right).

(12.53)

2.

Poincaré ball.
As a geodesic is of the form
\gamma(t)=\tanh\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)p
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we deduce from
Proposition

4.1
that

s(x)=\tanh\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)\iff t=2\arctanh\big(s(x)\big).

(12.54)

∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.3

Proof of
Proposition

4.3
.

1.

Lorentz model.

The geodesic in direction
v
can be characterized by

\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\ \gamma_{v}(t)=\cosh(t)x^{0}+\sinh(t)v.

(12.55)

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{L}^{d},\ d_{\mathbb{L}}(\gamma_{v}(t),x)

\displaystyle=\arccosh(-\cosh(t)\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}-\sinh(t)\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}})

(12.56)

\displaystyle=\arccosh\left(-\frac{e^{t}+e^{-t}}{2}\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}-\frac{e^{t}-e^{-t}}{2}\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}\right)

\displaystyle=\arccosh\left(\frac{e^{t}}{2}\big((-1-e^{-2t})\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}+(-1+e^{-2t})\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}\big)\right)

\displaystyle=\arccosh\big(x(t)\big).

Then, on one hand, we have
x(t)\underset{t\to\infty}{\to}\pm\infty
, and using that
\arccosh(x)=\log\big(x+\sqrt{x^{2}-1}\big)
, we have

\displaystyle d_{\mathbb{L}}(\gamma_{v}(t),x)-t

\displaystyle=\log\left(\big(x(t)+\sqrt{x(t)^{2}-1}\big)e^{-t}\right)

(12.57)

\displaystyle=\log\left(e^{-t}x(t)+e^{-t}x(t)\sqrt{1-\frac{1}{x(t)^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\underset{\infty}{=}\log\left(e^{-t}x(t)+e^{-t}x(t)\left(1-\frac{1}{2x(t)^{2}}+o\left(\frac{1}{x(t)^{2}}\right)\right)\right).

Moreover,

e^{-t}x(t)=\frac{1}{2}(-1-e^{-2t})\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}+\frac{1}{2}(-1+e^{-2t})\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}\underset{t\to\infty}{\to}-\frac{1}{2}\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}.

(12.58)

Hence,

B^{v}(x)=\log(-\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}).

(12.59)

2.

Poincaré ball.

Note that this proof can be found
e.g.
in the Appendix of
(
Ghadimi Atigh et al. 2021
)
. We report it for the sake of completeness.

Let
p\in S^{d-1}
, then the geodesic from
0
to
p
is of the form
\gamma_{p}(t)=\exp_{0}(tp)=\tanh(\frac{t}{2})p
.
Moreover, recall that
\arccosh(x)=\log(x+\sqrt{x^{2}-1})
and

d_{\mathbb{B}}(\gamma_{p}(t),x)=\arccosh\left(1+2\frac{\|\tanh(\frac{t}{2})p-x\|_{2}^{2}}{(1-\tanh^{2}(\frac{t}{2}))(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})}\right)=\arccosh(1+x(t)),

(12.60)

where

x(t)=2\frac{\|\tanh(\frac{t}{2})p-x\|_{2}^{2}}{(1-\tanh^{2}(\frac{t}{2}))(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})}.

(12.61)

Now, on one hand, we have

\displaystyle B^{p}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\ (d_{\mathbb{B}}(\gamma_{p}(t),x)-t)

(12.62)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\ \log\big(1+x(t)+\sqrt{x(t)^{2}+2x(t)}\big)-t

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\ \log\big(e^{-t}(1+x(t)+\sqrt{x(t)^{2}+2x(t)})\big).

On the other hand, using that
\tanh(\frac{t}{2})=\frac{e^{t}-1}{e^{t}+1}
,

\displaystyle e^{-t}x(t)

\displaystyle=2e^{-t}\frac{\|\frac{e^{t}-1}{e^{t}+1}p-x\|_{2}^{2}}{(1-(\frac{e^{t}-1}{e^{t}+1})^{2})(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})}

(12.63)

\displaystyle=2e^{-t}\frac{\|e^{t}p-p-e^{t}x-x\|_{2}^{2}}{4e^{t}(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\|p-e^{-t}p-x-e^{-t}x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle\underset{t\to\infty}{\to}\frac{1}{2}\frac{\|p-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle B^{p}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\ \log\left(e^{-t}+e^{-t}x(t)+e^{-t}x(t)\sqrt{1+\frac{2}{x(t)}}\right)=\log\left(\frac{\|p-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}\right),

(12.64)

using that
\sqrt{1+\frac{2}{x(t)}}=1+\frac{1}{x(t)}+o(\frac{1}{x(t)})
and
\frac{1}{x(t)}\to_{t\to\infty}0
.

∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.4

Proof of
Proposition

4.4
.

1.

Lorentz model.

First, a point on the geodesic
\gamma_{v}
is of the form

y(t)=\cosh(t)x^{0}+\sinh(t)v,

(12.65)

with
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

The projection along the horosphere amounts at following the level sets of the Busemann function
B^{v}
. And we have

\displaystyle B^{v}(x)=B^{v}(y(t))

\displaystyle\iff\log(-\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}})=\log(-\langle\cosh(t)x^{0}+\sinh(t)v,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}})

(12.66)

\displaystyle\iff\log(-\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}})=\log(-\cosh(t)\|x^{0}\|_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}-\sinh(t)\|v\|_{\mathbb{L}}^{2})

\displaystyle\iff\log(-\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}=\log(\cosh(t)-\sinh(t))

\displaystyle\iff\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}=\sinh(t)-\cosh(t).

By noticing that
\cosh(t)=\frac{1+\tanh^{2}(\frac{t}{2})}{1-\tanh^{2}(\frac{t}{2})}
and
\sinh(t)=\frac{2\tanh(\frac{t}{2})}{1-\tanh^{2}(\frac{t}{2})}
, let
u=\tanh(\frac{t}{2})
, then we have

\displaystyle B^{v}(x)=B^{v}(y(t))

\displaystyle\iff\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}=\frac{2u}{1-u^{2}}-\frac{1+u^{2}}{1-u^{2}}=\frac{-(u-1)^{2}}{(1-u)(1+u)}=\frac{u-1}{u+1}

(12.67)

\displaystyle\iff u=\frac{1+\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}{1-\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}.

We can further continue the computation and obtain, by denoting
c=\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{B}^{v}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+u^{2}}{1-u^{2}}x^{0}+\frac{2u}{1-u^{2}}v

(12.68)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\left(\frac{1+c}{1-c}\right)^{2}}{1-\left(\frac{1+c}{1-c}\right)^{2}}x^{0}+2\frac{\left(\frac{1+c}{1-c}\right)}{1-\left(\frac{1+c}{1-c}\right)^{2}}v

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-c)^{2}+(1+c)^{2}}{(1-c)^{2}-(1+c)^{2}}x^{0}+2\frac{(1+c)(1-c)}{(1-c)^{2}-(1+c)^{2}}v

\displaystyle=-\frac{1+c^{2}}{2c}x^{0}-\frac{1-c^{2}}{2c}v

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}\big((1+\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2})x^{0}+(1-\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2})v\big).

2.

Poincaré ball.

Let
p\in S^{d-1}
. First, we notice that points on the geodesic generated by
p
and passing through 0 are of the form
x(\lambda)=\lambda p
where
\lambda\in]-1,1[
.

Moreover, there is a unique horosphere
S(p,x)
passing through
x
and starting from
p
. The points on this horosphere are of the form

\displaystyle y(\theta)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{p+x(\lambda^{*})}{2}\right)+\left\|\frac{p-x(\lambda^{*})}{2}\right\|_{2}\left(\cos(\theta)p+\sin(\theta)\frac{x-\langle x,p\rangle p}{\|x-\langle x,p\rangle p\|_{2}}\right)

(12.69)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\lambda^{*}}{2}p+\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{2}\left(\cos(\theta)p+\sin(\theta)\frac{x-\langle x,p\rangle p}{\|x-\langle x,p\rangle p\|_{2}}\right),

where
\lambda^{*}
characterizes the intersection between the geodesic and the horosphere.

Since the horosphere are the level sets of the Busemann function, we have
B^{p}(x)=B^{p}(\lambda^{*}p)
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle B^{p}(x)=B^{p}(\lambda^{*}p)

\displaystyle\iff\log\left(\frac{\|p-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}\right)=\log\left(\frac{\|p-\lambda^{*}p\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|\lambda^{*}p\|_{2}^{2}}\right)

(12.70)

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\|p-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}=\frac{(1-\lambda^{*})^{2}}{1-(\lambda^{*})^{2}}

\displaystyle\iff\frac{\|p-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}=\frac{1-\lambda^{*}}{1+\lambda^{*}}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda^{*}\left(\frac{\|p-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}+1\right)=1-\frac{\|p-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda^{*}=\frac{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}-\|p-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|p-x\|_{2}^{2}}.

∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.5

First, we show some Lemma.

Lemma 12.6

(Commutation of projections.)
.

Let
v\in\mathrm{span}(x^{0})^{\bot}\cap S^{d}
of the form
v=(0,\tilde{v})
where
\tilde{v}\in S^{d-1}
.
Then, for all
x\in\mathbb{B}^{d}
,
y\in\mathbb{L}^{d}
,

\displaystyle P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}\big(\tilde{B}^{\tilde{v}}(x)\big)=\tilde{B}^{v}\big(P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(x)\big),

(12.71)

\displaystyle\tilde{B}^{\tilde{v}}(P_{\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{B}}(y))=P_{\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{B}}(\tilde{B}^{v}(y))

(12.72)

\displaystyle P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}\big(\tilde{P}^{\tilde{v}}(x)\big)=\tilde{P}^{v}\big(P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(x)\big),

(12.73)

\displaystyle\tilde{P}^{\tilde{v}}(P_{\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{B}}(y))=P_{\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{B}}(\tilde{P}^{v}(y)).

(12.74)

Proof.

Proof of (
12.71
).
We first show (
12.71
). Let’s recall the formula of the different projections.

On one hand,

\forall x\in\mathbb{B}^{d},\ \tilde{B}^{\tilde{v}}(x)=\left(\frac{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}-\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}\right)\tilde{v},

(12.75)

\forall x\in\mathbb{L}^{d},\ \tilde{B}^{v}(x)=-\frac{1}{2\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}\big((1+\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2})x^{0}+(1-\langle x,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2})v\big),

(12.76)

and

\forall x\in\mathbb{B}^{d},\ P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(x)=\frac{1}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}(1+\|x\|_{2}^{2},2x_{1},\dots,2x_{d}).

(12.77)

Let
x\in\mathbb{B}^{d}
.
First, let’s compute
P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}\big(\tilde{B}^{\tilde{v}}(x)\big)
. We note that
\|\tilde{v}\|_{2}^{2}=1
and therefore

\|\tilde{B}^{\tilde{v}}(v)\|_{2}^{2}=\left(\frac{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}-\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}\right)^{2}.

(12.78)

Then,

\displaystyle P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}\big(\tilde{B}^{\tilde{v}}(x)\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\left(\frac{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}-\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}\right)^{2}}\left(1+\left(\frac{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}-\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}\right)^{2},2\left(\frac{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}-\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}\right)\tilde{v}\right)

(12.79)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\left(\frac{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}-\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}\right)^{2}}\left(\left(1+\left(\frac{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}-\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}\right)^{2}\right)x^{0}+2\left(\frac{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}-\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}\right)v\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\big(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{2}}{4\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})}\left(\frac{2(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})^{2}+2\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{4}}{\big(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{2}}x^{0}+2\left(\frac{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}-\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}}\right)v\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})}\left(\big((1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{4}\big)x^{0}+(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}-\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2})(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2})v\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{2}(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})}\left((1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{4}\big)x^{0}+\big((1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})^{2}-\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{4}\big)v\right).

Now, let’s compute
\tilde{B}^{v}\big(P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(x)\big)
. First, let’s remark that for all
y\in\mathbb{L}^{d}
,
\langle y,x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}=-y_{0}+\langle y_{1:d},\tilde{v}\rangle
. Therefore, for all
x\in\mathbb{B}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\langle P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(x),x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}

\displaystyle=\langle\frac{1}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}(1+\|x\|_{2}^{2},2x_{1},\dots,2x_{d}),x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}

(12.80)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}\left(-1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+2\langle x,\tilde{v}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}\|x-\tilde{v}\|_{2}^{2}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\langle P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(x),x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})^{2}}\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{4}.

(12.81)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{B}^{v}\big(P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(x)\big)

\displaystyle=\tilde{B}^{v}\left(\frac{1}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}(1+\|x\|_{2}^{2},2x_{1},\dots,2x_{d})\right)

(12.82)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\left(-1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}+2\langle x,\tilde{v}\rangle\right)}\left(\big(1+\langle P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(x),x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}\big)x^{0}+\big(1-\langle P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(x),x^{0}+v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}\big)v\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2\|x-\tilde{v}\|_{2}^{2}}\left(\frac{(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|^{4}}{(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})^{2}}x^{0}+\frac{(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})^{2}-\|\tilde{v}-x\|^{4}}{(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})^{2}}v\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\|x-\tilde{v}\|_{2}^{2}(1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})}\left((1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})^{2}+\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{4}\big)x^{0}+\big((1-\|x\|_{2}^{2})^{2}-\|\tilde{v}-x\|_{2}^{4}\big)v\right)

\displaystyle=P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}\big(\tilde{B}^{\tilde{v}}(x)\big).

Proof of (
12.72
).
For (
12.72
), we use that
P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}
and
P_{\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{B}}
are inverse from each other. Hence, for all
x\in\mathbb{B}^{d}
, there exists
y\in\mathbb{L}^{d}
such that
x=P_{\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{B}}(y)\iff y=P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(x)
, and we obtain the second equality by plugging it into (
12.71
).

Proof of (
12.73
) and (
12.74
).
Now, let’s show (
12.73
). The proof relies on the observation that
\{\exp_{x^{0}}(tv),\ t\in\mathbb{R}\}=P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}\left(\{\exp_{0}(t\tilde{v}),\ t\in\mathbb{R}\}\right)
(
i.e.
the images by
P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}
of geodesics in the Poincaré ball are geodesics in the Lorentz model). Thus,

\displaystyle\tilde{P}^{v}(P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(x))

\displaystyle=\argmin_{z\in\{\exp_{x^{0}}(tv),\ t\in\mathbb{R}\}}\ d_{\mathbb{L}}(P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(x),z)

(12.83)

\displaystyle=P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}\big(\argmin_{z\in\{\exp_{0}(t\tilde{v}),\ t\in\mathbb{R}\}}\ d_{\mathbb{B}}(P_{\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{L}}(x),P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(z))\big)

\displaystyle=P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}\big(\argmin_{z\in\{\exp_{0}(t\tilde{v}),\ t\in\mathbb{R}\}}\ d_{\mathbb{B}}(x,z)\big)

\displaystyle=P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}\big(\tilde{P}^{v}(x)\big).

Similarly, we obtain (
12.74
).
∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.5
.

Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{B}^{d})
,
\tilde{\mu}=(P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}})_{\#}\mu
,
\tilde{\nu}=(P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}})_{\#}\nu
,
\tilde{v}\in S^{d-1}
an ideal point and
v=(0,\tilde{v})\in\mathrm{span}(x^{0})^{\bot}
.

Then, using
Proposition

3.4
,
Lemma

12.1
, that
P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}:\mathbb{B}^{d}\to\mathbb{L}^{d}
is an isometry and Lemma
12.6
, we have:

\displaystyle W_{p}^{p}(B^{\tilde{v}}_{\#}\mu,B^{\tilde{v}}_{\#}\nu)

\displaystyle=W_{p}^{p}(\tilde{B}^{\tilde{v}}_{\#}\mu,\tilde{B}^{\tilde{v}}_{\#}\nu)\quad\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:eq_wasserstein_busemann}}

(12.84)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int_{\mathbb{B}^{d}\times\mathbb{B}^{d}}d_{\mathbb{B}}\big(\tilde{B}^{\tilde{v}}(x),\tilde{B}^{\tilde{v}}(y)\big)^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)\quad\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:paty}}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int_{\mathbb{B}^{d}\times\mathbb{B}^{d}}d_{\mathbb{L}}\big(P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(\tilde{B}^{\tilde{v}}(x)),P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(\tilde{B}^{\tilde{v}}(y))\big)^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)\quad\text{using that $P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}$ is an isometry}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int_{\mathbb{B}^{d}\times\mathbb{B}^{d}}d_{\mathbb{L}}\big(\tilde{B}^{v}(P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(x)),\tilde{B}^{v}(P_{\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{L}}(y))\big)^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)\quad\text{using \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:commute_projs}}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\tilde{B}^{v}_{\#}\tilde{\mu},\tilde{B}^{v}_{\#}\tilde{\nu})}\ \int_{\mathbb{L}^{d}\times\mathbb{L}^{d}}d_{\mathbb{L}}(x,y)^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)\quad\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:paty}}

\displaystyle=W_{p}^{p}(\tilde{B}^{v}_{\#}\tilde{\mu},\tilde{B}^{v}_{\#}\tilde{\nu})

\displaystyle=W_{p}^{p}(B^{v}_{\#}\tilde{\mu},B^{v}_{\#}\tilde{\nu})\quad\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:eq_wasserstein_busemann}}.

It is true for all
\tilde{v}\in S^{d-1}
, and hence for
\lambda
-almost all
\tilde{v}\in S^{d-1}
. Therefore, we have

\mathrm{HHSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)=\mathrm{HHSW}_{p}^{p}(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\nu}).

(12.85)

Using the same proof with
Proposition

3.3
instead of
Proposition

3.4
and
P^{v}
instead of
B^{v}
, we obtain

\mathrm{GHSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)=\mathrm{GHSW}_{p}^{p}(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\nu}).

(12.86)

∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.6

Proof of
Proposition

4.6
.

We will prove this proposition directly by working on the geodesics. As
t^{v}
is a isometry (
Proposition

3.1
), for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a unique
z
on the geodesic
\mathrm{span}(x^{0},v)\cap\mathbb{L}^{d}
such that
t=t^{v}(z)
, and we can rewrite the set of integration as

\{x\in\mathbb{L}^{d},\ P^{v}(x)=t\}=\{x\in\mathbb{L}^{d},\ \tilde{P}^{v}(x)=z\}.

(12.87)

For the first inclusion, let
x\in\{x\in\mathbb{L}^{d},\ \tilde{P}^{v}(x)=z\}
. By Proposition
4.1
and hypothesis, we have that

\tilde{P}^{v}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}-\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}}\big(-\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}x^{0}+\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}v\big)=z.

(12.88)

Let’s denote
E=\mathrm{span}(v,x^{0})
the plan generating the geodesic. Then, by denoting
P^{E}
the orthogonal projection on
E
, we have

\displaystyle P^{E}(x)

\displaystyle=\langle x,v\rangle v+\langle x,x^{0}\rangle x^{0}

(12.89)

\displaystyle=\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}v-\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}x^{0}

\displaystyle=\left(\sqrt{\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}-\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}\right)z,

using that
v_{0}=0
since
\langle x^{0},v\rangle=v_{0}=0
, and hence
\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}=\langle x,v\rangle
, that
\langle x,x^{0}\rangle=x_{0}=-\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}
and (
12.88
).
Then, since
v_{z}\in\mathrm{span}(v,x^{0})
and
\langle z,v_{z}\rangle=0
(by construction of
R_{z}
), we have

\displaystyle\langle x,v_{z}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle P^{E}(x),v_{z}\rangle

(12.90)

\displaystyle=\langle\left(\sqrt{\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}-\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}\right)z,v_{z}\rangle=0.

Thus,
x\in\mathrm{span}(v_{z})^{\bot}\cap\mathbb{L}^{d}
.

For the second inclusion, let
x\in\mathrm{span}(v_{z})^{\bot}\cap\mathbb{L}^{d}
. Since
z\in\mathrm{span}(v_{z})^{\bot}
(by construction of
R_{z}
), we can decompose
\mathrm{span}(v_{z})^{\bot}
as
\mathrm{span}(v_{z})^{\bot}=\mathrm{span}(z)\oplus(\mathrm{span}(z)^{\bot}\setminus\mathrm{span}(v_{z}))
. Hence, there exists
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such that
x=\lambda z+x^{\bot}
. Moreover, as
z\in\mathrm{span}(x^{0},v)
, we have
\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}=\lambda\langle z,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}
and
\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}=\langle x,v\rangle=\lambda\langle z,v\rangle=\lambda\langle z,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}
.
Thus, the projection is

\displaystyle\tilde{P}^{v}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}-\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}}\big(-\langle x,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}x^{0}+\langle x,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}v\big)

(12.91)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{|\lambda|}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\langle z,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}-\langle z,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}}\big(-\langle z,x^{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}x^{0}+\langle z,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}v\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{|\lambda|}z=\mathrm{sign}(\lambda)z.

But,
-z\notin\mathbb{L}^{d}
, hence necessarily,
\tilde{P}^{v}(x)=z
.

Finally, we can conclude that
\{x\in\mathbb{L}^{d},\ \tilde{P}^{v}(x)=z\}=\mathrm{span}(v_{z})^{\bot}\cap\mathbb{L}^{d}
.
∎

12.2.2
Details on Hyperbolic Spaces

In this Section, we first recall different generalizations of the Gaussian distribution on Hyperbolic spaces, with a particular focus on Wrapped normal distributions. Then, we recall how to perform Riemannian gradient descent in the Lorentz model and in the Poincaré ball.

Distributions on Hyperbolic Spaces

Riemannian normal.

The first way of naturally generalizing Gaussian distributions to Riemannian manifolds is to use the geodesic distance in the density, which becomes

f(x)\propto\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}d_{M}(x,\mu)^{2}\right).

It is actually the distribution maximizing the entropy
(
Pennec 2006
,
Said et al. 2014
)
. However, it is not straightforward to sample from such a distribution. For example,
Ovinnikov 2019
uses a rejection sampling algorithm.

Wrapped normal distribution.

A more convenient distribution, on which we can use the parameterization trick, is the Wrapped normal distribution
(
Nagano et al. 2019
)
. This distribution can be sampled from by first drawing
v\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
and then transforming it into
v\in T_{x^{0}}\mathbb{L}^{d}
by concatenating a 0 in the first coordinate. Then, we perform parallel transport to transport
v
from the tangent space of
x^{0}
to the tangent space of
\mu\in\mathbb{L}^{d}
. Finally, we can project the samples on the manifold using the exponential map. We recall the formula of parallel transport form
x
to
y
:

\forall v\in T_{x}\mathbb{L}^{d},\ \mathrm{PT}_{x\to y}(v)=v+\frac{\langle y,v\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}{1-\langle x,y\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}}(x+y).

(12.92)

Since it only involves differentiable operations, we can perform the parameterization trick and
e.g.
optimize directly over the mean and the variance. Moreover, by the change of variable formula, we can also derive the density
(
Nagano et al. 2019
,
Bose et al. 2020
)
. Let
\tilde{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
,
z=(0,\tilde{z})\in T_{x^{0}}\mathbb{L}^{d}
,
u=\mathrm{PT}_{x^{0}\to\mu}(z)
, then the density of
x=\exp_{\mu}(u)
is:

\log p(x)=\log p(\tilde{z})-(d-1)\log\left(\frac{\sinh(\|u\|_{\mathbb{L}})}{\|u\|_{\mathbb{L}}}\right).

(12.93)

In the paper, we write
x\sim\mathcal{G}(\mu,\Sigma)
.

Optimization on Hyperbolic Spaces

For gradient descent on hyperbolic space, we refer to
(
Boumal 2023
, Section 7.6)
and
(
Wilson and Leimeister 2018
)
.

In general, for a functional
f:M\to\mathbb{R}
, Riemannian gradient descent is performed, analogously to the Euclidean space, by following the geodesics. Hence, the gradient descent reads as
(
Absil et al. 2009
,
Bonnabel 2013
)

\forall k\geq 0,\ x_{k+1}=\exp_{x_{k}}\big(-\gamma\mathrm{grad}f(x_{k})\big).

(12.94)

Note that the exponential map can be replaced more generally by a retraction. We describe in the following paragraphs the different formulae in the Lorentz model and in the Poincaré ball.

Lorentz model.

Let
f:\mathbb{L}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, then its Riemannian gradient is
(
Boumal 2023
, Proposition 7.7)

\mathrm{grad}f(x)=\mathrm{Proj}_{x}(J\nabla f(x)),

(12.95)

where
J=\mathrm{diag}(-1,1,\dots,1)
and
\mathrm{Proj}_{x}(z)=z+\langle x,z\rangle_{\mathbb{L}}x
. Furthermore, the exponential map is

\forall v\in T_{x}\mathbb{L}^{d},\ \exp_{x}(v)=\cosh(\|v\|_{\mathbb{L}})x+\sinh(\|v\|_{\mathbb{L}})\frac{v}{\|v\|_{\mathbb{L}}}.

(12.96)

Poincaré ball.

On
\mathbb{B}^{d}
, the Riemannian gradient of
f:\mathbb{B}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
can be obtained as
(
Nickel and Kiela 2017
, Section 3)

\mathrm{grad}f(x)=\frac{(1-\|\theta\|_{2}^{2})^{2}}{4}\nabla f(x).

(12.97)

Nickel and Kiela 2017
propose to use as retraction
R_{x}(v)=x+v
instead of the exponential map, and add a projection, to constrain the value to remain within the Poincaré ball, of the form

\mathrm{proj}(x)=\begin{cases}\frac{x}{\|x\|_{2}}-\epsilon\quad\text{if }\|x\|\geq 1\\
x\quad\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(12.98)

where
\epsilon=10^{-5}
is a small constant ensuring numerical stability. Hence, the algorithm becomes

x_{k+1}=\mathrm{proj}\left(x_{k}-\gamma_{k}\frac{(1-\|x_{k}\|_{2}^{2})^{2}}{4}\nabla f(x_{k})\right).

(12.99)

A second solution is to compute directly the exponential map derived in
(
Ganea et al. 2018b
, Corollary 1.1)
:

\exp_{x}(v)=\frac{\lambda_{x}\big(\cosh(\lambda_{x}\|v\|_{2})+\langle x,\frac{v}{\|v\|_{2}}\rangle\sinh(\lambda_{x}\|v\|_{2})\big)x+\frac{1}{\|v\|_{2}}\sinh(\lambda_{x}\|v\|_{2})v}{1+(\lambda_{x}-1)\cosh(\lambda_{x}\|v\|_{2})+\lambda_{x}\langle x,\frac{v}{\|v\|_{2}}\rangle\sinh(\lambda_{x}\|v\|_{2})},

(12.100)

where
\lambda_{x}=\frac{2}{1-\|x\|_{2}^{2}}
.

12.2.3
Additional Details of Experiments

Gradient flows

Denoting
\nu
the target distribution from which we have access to samples
(y_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
, we aim at learning
\nu
by solving the following optimization problem:

\mu=\argmin_{\mu}\ \mathrm{HSW}\left(\mu,\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\delta_{x_{i}}\right).

(12.101)

As we cannot directly learn
\mu
, we model it as
\hat{\mu}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
, and then learn the sample locations
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
using a Riemannian gradient descent which we described in Appendix
12.2.2
. In practice, we take
n=500
and use batches of
500
target samples at each iteration. To compute the sliced discrepancies, we always use 1000 projections. On Figure
4.4
, we plot the log 2-Wasserstein with geodesic cost between the model measure
\hat{\mu}_{k}
at each iteration
k
and
\nu
. We average over 5 runs of each gradient descent. Now, we describe the specific setting for the different targets.

Wrapped normal distribution.

For the first experiment, we choose as target a wrapped normal distribution
\mathcal{G}(m,\Sigma)
. In the fist setting, we use
m=(1.5,1.25,0)\in\mathbb{L}^{2}
and
\Sigma=0.1I_{2}
. In the second, we use
m=(8,\sqrt{63},0)\in\mathbb{L}^{2}
and
\Sigma=0.1I_{2}
. The learning rate is fixed as 5 for the different discrepancies, except for SWl on the second WND which lies far from origin, and for which we exhibit numerical instabilities with a learning rate too high. Hence, we reduced it to 0.1. We observed the same issue for HHSW on the Lorentz model. Fortunately, the Poincaré version, which is equal to the Lorentz version, did not suffer from these issues. It underlines the benefit of having both formulations.

Mixture of wrapped normal distributions.

For the second experiment, the target is a mixture of 5 WNDs. The covariance are all taken equal as
0.01I_{2}
. For the first setting, the outlying means are (on the Poincaré ball)
m_{1}=(0,-0.5)
,
m_{2}=(0,0.5)
,
m_{3}=(0.5,0)
,
m_{4}=(-0.5,0)
and the center mean is
m_{5}=(0,0.1)
. In the second setting, the outlying means are
m_{1}=(0,-0.9)
,
m_{2}=(0,0.9)
,
m_{3}=(0.9,0)
and
m_{4}=(-0.9,0)
. We use the same
m_{5}
. The learning rate in this experiment is fixed at 1 for all discrepancies.

Classification of Images with Busemann

Denote
\{(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\}
the training set where
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
y_{i}\in\{1,\dots,C\}
is a label. The embedding is performed by using a neural network
f_{\theta}
and the exponential map at the last layer, which projects the points on the Poincaré ball,
i.e.
for
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, the embedding of
x_{i}
is
z_{i}=\exp_{0}\big(f_{\theta}(z_{i})\big)
, where
\exp_{0}
is given by (
12.100
), or more simply by

\exp_{0}(x)=\tanh\left(\frac{\|x\|_{2}}{2}\right)\frac{x}{\|x\|_{2}}.

(12.102)

The experimental setting of this experiment is the same as
(
Ghadimi Atigh et al. 2021
)
. That is, we use a Resnet-32 backbone and optimize it with Adam
(
Kingma and Ba 2015
)
, a learning rate of 5e-4, weight decay of 5e-5, batch size of 128 and without pre-training. The network is trained for all experiments for 1110 epochs with learning rate decay of 10 after 1000 and 1100 epochs. Moreover, the
C
prototypes are given by the algorithm of
(
Mettes et al. 2019
)
and are uniform on the sphere
S^{d-1}
.

For the additional hyperparameters in the loss (
4.31
), we use by default
\lambda=1
, and a mixture of
C
wrapped normal distributions with means
\alpha p_{c}
, where
p_{c}\in S^{d-1}
is a prototype,
c\in\{1,\dots,C\}
and
\alpha=0.75
, and covariance matrix
\sigma I_{d}
with
\sigma=0.1
. For CIFAR100, in dimension 50, we use
\lambda=1
,
\sigma=0.01
and
\alpha=0.1
.

12.3
Appendix of
Chapter

5

12.3.1
Proofs of
Section

5.3

Proof of
Proposition

5.1

Proof of
Proposition

5.1
.

Let
M\in S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})
. We want to solve

\tilde{P}^{\mathcal{G}_{A}}(M)=\argmin_{X\in\mathcal{G}_{A}}\ d_{LE}(X,M)^{2}.

(12.103)

In the case of the Log-Euclidean metric,
\mathcal{G}_{A}=\{\exp(tA),\ t\in\mathbb{R}\}
.
We have

\displaystyle d_{LE}(\exp(tA),M)^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\log\exp(tA)-\log M\|_{F}^{2}

(12.104)

\displaystyle=\|tA-\log M\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=t^{2}\mathrm{Tr}(A^{2})+\mathrm{Tr}(\log(M)^{2})-2t\mathrm{Tr}(A\log M)

\displaystyle=g(t).

Hence

g^{\prime}(t)=0\iff t=\frac{\mathrm{Tr}(A\log M)}{\mathrm{Tr}(A^{2})}.

(12.105)

Therefore

\tilde{P}^{\mathcal{G}_{A}}(M)=\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{Tr}(A\log M)}{\mathrm{Tr}(A^{2})}A\right)=\exp\left(\mathrm{Tr}(A\log M)A\right),

(12.106)

since
\|A\|_{F}^{2}=\mathrm{Tr}(A^{2})=1
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

5.2

Proof of
Proposition

5.2
.

First, we give an orientation to the geodesic. This can be done by taking the sign of the inner product between
\log(\tilde{P}^{\mathcal{G}_{A}}(M))
and
A
.

\displaystyle P^{A}(M)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}(\langle A,\log(\tilde{P}^{\mathcal{G}_{A}}(M))\rangle_{F})d\big(\tilde{P}^{A}(M),I\big)

(12.107)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}(\langle A,\log(\tilde{P}^{\mathcal{G}_{A}}(M))\rangle_{F})d\left(\exp\left(\mathrm{Tr}(A\log M)A\right),I\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}(\langle A,\langle A,\log M\rangle_{F}A\rangle_{F})\|\langle A\log M\rangle_{F}A-\log I\|_{F}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}(\langle A,\log M\rangle_{F})|\langle A,\log M\rangle_{F}|

\displaystyle=\langle A,\log M\rangle_{F}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(A\log M).

∎

Proof of
Proposition

5.3

Proof of
Proposition

5.3
.

First, following
(
Bridson and Haefliger 2013
)
, we have for all
M\in S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R}),

B^{A}(M)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\ \big(d_{LE}(\gamma_{A}(t),M)-t\big)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{d_{LE}(\gamma_{A}(t),M)^{2}-t^{2}}{2t},

(12.108)

denoting
\gamma_{A}:t\mapsto\exp(tA)
is the geodesic line associated to
\mathcal{G}_{A}
. Then, we get

\displaystyle\frac{d_{LE}(\gamma_{A}(t),M)^{2}-t^{2}}{2t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2t}\left(\|\log\gamma_{A}(t)-\log M\|_{F}^{2}-t^{2}\right)

(12.109)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2t}\left(\|tA-\log M\|_{F}^{2}-t^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2t}\left(t^{2}\|A\|_{F}^{2}+\|\log M\|_{F}^{2}-2t\langle A,\log M\rangle_{F}-t^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-\langle A,\log M\rangle_{F}+\frac{1}{2t}\|\log M\|_{F}^{2},

using that
\|A\|_{F}=1
. Then, by passing to the limit
t\to\infty
, we find

B^{A}(t)=-\langle A,\log M\rangle_{F}=-\mathrm{Tr}(A\log M).

(12.110)

∎

Proof of
Proposition

5.4

Proof of
Proposition

5.4
.

Denoting
t^{A}(B)=\langle B,A\rangle_{F}
for all
B\in S_{d}(\mathbb{R})
, we obtain using
Lemma

12.1

\displaystyle W_{p}^{p}(t^{A}_{\#}\log_{\#}\mu,t^{A}_{\#}\log_{\#}\nu)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})\times S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}|t^{A}(\log(X))-t^{A}(\log(Y))|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(X,Y)

(12.111)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})\times S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}|P^{A}(X)-P^{A}(Y)|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(X,Y)

\displaystyle=W_{p}^{p}(P^{A}_{\#}\mu,P^{A}_{\#}\nu),

since
t^{A}(\log X)=\langle A,\log X\rangle_{F}=P^{A}(X)
. Hence,

\mathrm{SymSW}_{p}^{p}(\log_{\#}\mu,\log_{\#}\nu)=\mathrm{SPDSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)\kern 5.0pt.

(12.112)

∎

Proof of
Lemma

5.1

Proof of
Lemma

5.1
.

A matrix in
S_{d}(\mathbb{R})
has a unique decomposition
P\mathrm{diag}(\theta)P^{T}
up to permutations of the columns of
P\in\mathcal{O}_{d}
and coefficients of
\theta\in S^{d-1}
.
Thus, there is a bijection between
\{A\in S_{d}(\mathbb{R}),\ \|A\|_{F}=1\}
and the set
S_{\mathcal{(}O),S^{d-1}}
of
d!
-tuple
\{(P_{1},\theta_{1}),\dots,(P_{d!},\theta_{d!})\in(\mathcal{O}_{d}\times S^{d-1})^{d!}\}
such that
(P_{i},\theta_{i})
is a permutation of
(P_{j},\theta_{j})
.
Therefore, the uniform distribution
\lambda_{S_{\mathcal{(}O),S^{d-1}}}
on
S_{\mathcal{(}O),S^{d-1}}
, defined as
\mathrm{d}\lambda_{S_{\mathcal{(}O),S^{d-1}}}((P_{1},\theta_{1}),\dots,(P_{d!},\theta_{d!}))=\sum_{i=1}^{n!}\mathrm{d}(\lambda_{O}\otimes\lambda)(P_{i},\theta_{i})=d!\cdot\mathrm{d}(\lambda_{O}\otimes\lambda)(P_{1},\theta_{1})
, allows to define a uniform distribution
\lambda_{S}
on
\{A\in S_{d}(\mathbb{R}),\ \|A\|_{F}=1\}
. Let
A=P\mathrm{diag}\theta P^{T}
with
(P,\theta)\in\mathcal{O}_{d}\times S^{d-1}
, then

\mathrm{d}\lambda_{S}(A)=d!\ \mathrm{d}(\lambda_{O}\otimes\lambda)(P,\theta).

(12.113)

∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.1

Proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

By
Proposition

3.5
, we know that
\mathrm{SPDSW}
is a finite pseudo-distance on
\mathcal{P}_{p}(S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R}))
. We need here to show indiscernible property.

Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R}))
such that
\mathrm{SPDSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)=0
. Then, as for all
A\in S_{d}(\mathbb{R})
,
W_{p}^{p}(P^{A}_{\#}\mu,P^{A}_{\#}\nu)\geq 0
, it implies that for
\lambda_{S}
-almost every
A
,
W_{p}^{p}(P^{A}_{\#}\mu,P^{A}_{\#}\nu)=0
which implies
P^{A}_{\#}\mu=P^{A}_{\#}\nu
for
\lambda_{S}
-almost every
A
since
W_{p}
is a distance. By taking the Fourier transform, this implies that for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
\widehat{P^{A}_{\#}\mu}(s)=\widehat{P^{A}_{\#}\nu}(s)
. But, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{P^{A}_{\#}\mu}(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2i\pi ts}\ \mathrm{d}(P^{A}_{\#}\mu)(s)

(12.114)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}e^{-2i\pi P^{A}(M)s}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(M)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}e^{-2i\pi\langle sA,\log M\rangle_{F}}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(M)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{d}(\mathbb{R})}e^{-2i\pi\langle sA,S\rangle_{F}}\ \mathrm{d}(\log_{\#}\mu)(S)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\log_{\#}\mu}(sA).

Hence, we get that
\mathrm{SPDSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)=0
implies that for
\lambda_{S}
-almost every
A
,

\forall s\in\mathbb{R},\ \widehat{\log_{\#}\mu}(sA)=\widehat{P^{A}_{\#}\mu}(s)=\widehat{P^{A}_{\#}\nu}(s)=\widehat{\log_{\#}\nu}(sA).

(12.115)

By injectivity of the Fourier transform on
S_{d}(\mathbb{R})
, we get
\log_{\#}\mu=\log_{\#}\nu
. Then, as
\log
is a bijection from
S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})
to
S_{d}(\mathbb{R})
, we have for all Borelian
C\subset S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle\mu(C)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}\mathbb{1}_{C}(X)\ \mathrm{d}\mu(X)

(12.116)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{d}(\mathbb{R})}\mathbb{1}_{C}(\exp(S))\ \mathrm{d}(\log_{\#}\mu)(S)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{d}(\mathbb{R})}\mathbb{1}_{C}(\exp(S))\ \mathrm{d}(\log_{\#}\nu)(S)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}\mathbb{1}_{C}(Y)\ \mathrm{d}\nu(Y)

\displaystyle=\nu(C).

Hence, we conclude that
\mu=\nu
and that
\mathrm{SPDSW}_{p}
is a distance.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.2

To prove
Theorem

5.2
, we will adapt the proof of
Nadjahi et al. 2020b
to our projection. First, we start to adapt
Nadjahi et al. 2020b
:

Lemma 12.7

(Lemma S1 in
Nadjahi et al. 2020b
)
.

Let
(\mu_{k})_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R}))
and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R}))
such that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\ \mathrm{SPDSW}_{1}(\mu_{k},\mu)=0
. Then, there exists
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
non decreasing such that
\mu_{\varphi(k)}\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{\mathcal{L}}\mu
.

Proof.

By
Bogachev and Ruas 2007
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}\ \int_{S^{d}(\mathbb{R})}W_{1}(P^{A}_{\#}\mu_{k},P^{A}_{\#}\mu)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda_{S}(A)=0

(12.117)

implies that there exists a subsequence
(\mu_{\varphi(k)})_{k}
such that for
\lambda_{S}
-almost every
A\in S_{d}(\mathbb{R})
,

W_{1}(P^{A}_{\#}\mu_{\varphi(k)},P^{A}_{\#}\mu)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}0.

(12.118)

As the Wasserstein distance metrizes the weak convergence, this is equivalent to
P^{A}_{\#}\mu_{\varphi(k)}\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{\mathcal{L}}P^{A}_{\#}\mu
.

Then, by Levy’s characterization theorem, this is equivalent with the pointwise convergence of the characterization function,
i.e.
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\phi_{P^{A}_{\#}\mu_{\varphi(k)}}(t)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}\phi_{P^{A}_{\#}\mu}(t)
.
Moreover, we have for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{P^{A}_{\#}\mu_{\varphi(k)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-its}\mathrm{d}(P^{A}_{\#}\mu_{\varphi(k)})(t)

(12.119)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}e^{-iP^{A}(M)s}\ \mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi(k)}(M)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}e^{-i\langle sA,\log M\rangle_{F}}\ \mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi(k)}(M)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{d}(\mathbb{R})}e^{-i\langle sA,S\rangle_{F}}\ \mathrm{d}(\log_{\#}\mu_{\varphi(k)})(S)

\displaystyle=\phi_{\log_{\#}\mu_{\varphi(k)}}(sA)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}\phi_{\log_{\#}\mu}(sA).

Then, working in
S_{d}(\mathbb{R})
with the Frobenius norm, we can use the same proof of
Nadjahi et al. 2020b
by using a convolution with a gaussian kernel and show that it implies that
\log_{\#}\mu_{\varphi(k)}\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{\mathcal{L}}\log_{\#}\mu
.

Finally, let’s show that it implies the weak convergence of
(\mu_{\varphi(k)})_{k}
towards
\mu
. Let
f\in C_{b}(S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R}))
, then

\displaystyle\int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}f\ \mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi(k)}

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{d}(\mathbb{R})}f\circ\exp\ \mathrm{d}(\log_{\#}\mu_{\varphi(k)})

(12.120)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}\int_{S_{d}(\mathbb{R})}f\circ\exp\ \mathrm{d}(\log_{\#}\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}f\ \mathrm{d}\mu.

Hence, we an conclude that
\mu_{\varphi(k)}\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{\mathcal{L}}\mu
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.2
.

First, we suppose that
\mu_{k}\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{\mathcal{L}}\mu
in
\mathcal{P}_{p}(S_{d}(\mathbb{R}))
. Then, by continuity, we have that for
\lambda_{S}
almost every
A\in S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})
,
P^{A}_{\#}\mu_{k}\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}P^{A}_{\#}\mu
. Moreover, as the Wasserstein distance on
\mathbb{R}
metrizes the weak convergence,
W_{p}(P^{A}_{\#}\mu_{k},P^{A}_{\#}\mu)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}0
. Finally, as
W_{p}
is bounded and it converges for
\lambda_{S}
-almost every
A
, we have by the Lebesgue convergence dominated theorem that
\mathrm{SPDSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu_{k},\mu)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}0
.

On the other hand, suppose that
\mathrm{SPDSW}_{p}(\mu_{k},\mu)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}0
. We first adapt Lemma S1 of
(
Nadjahi et al. 2020b
)
in Lemma
12.7
and observe that by the Hölder inequality,

\mathrm{SPDSW}_{1}(\mu,\nu)\leq\mathrm{SPDSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu),

(12.121)

and hence
\mathrm{SPDSW}_{1}(\mu_{k},\mu)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}0
.

By the same contradiction argument as in
Nadjahi et al. 2020b
, let’s suppose that
(\mu_{k})_{k}
does not converge to
\mu
. Then, by denoting
d_{P}
the Lévy-Prokhorov metric,
\lim_{k\to\infty}d_{P}(\mu_{k},\mu)\neq 0
. Hence, there exists
\epsilon>0
and a subsequence
(\mu_{\varphi(k)})_{k}
such that
d_{P}(\mu_{\varphi(k)},\mu)>\epsilon
.

Then, we have first that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathrm{SPDSW}_{1}(\mu_{\varphi(k)},\mu)=0
. Thus, by Lemma
12.7
, there exists a subsequence
(\mu_{\psi(\varphi(k))})_{k}
such that
\mu_{\psi(\varphi(k))}\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{\mathcal{L}}\mu
which is equivalent to
\lim_{k\to\infty}d_{P}(\mu_{\psi(\varphi(k))},\mu)=0
which contradicts the hypothesis.

We conclude that
(\mu_{k})_{k}
converges weakly to
\mu
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.3

For the proof of
Theorem

5.3
, we will first recall the following Theorem:

Theorem 12.1

(
(
Rivin 2007
)
, Theorem 3)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
a homogeneous function of degree
p
(
i.e.

\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{R},\ f(\alpha x)=\alpha^{p}f(x)
). Then,

\Gamma\Big(\frac{d+p}{2}\Big)\int_{S^{d-1}}f(x)\ \lambda(\mathrm{d}x)=\Gamma\Big(\frac{d}{2}\Big)\mathbb{E}[f(X)]\kern 5.0pt,

(12.122)

where
\forall i\in\{1,...,d\}
,
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
(X_{i})_{i}
are independent.

Then, making extensive use of this theorem, we show the following lemma:

Lemma 12.8
.

\forall S\in S_{d}(\mathbb{R}),\ \int_{S^{d-1}}|\langle\mathrm{diag}(\theta),S\rangle_{F}|^{p}\ \lambda(\mathrm{d}\theta)=\frac{1}{d}\left(\sum_{i}S_{ii}^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\int_{S^{d-1}}\|\theta\|_{p}^{p}\ \lambda(\mathrm{d}\theta).

(12.123)

Proof.

Let
f:\theta\mapsto\|\theta\|_{p}^{p}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\theta_{i}^{p}
, then we have
f(\alpha\theta)=\alpha^{p}f(\theta)
and
f
is
p
-homogeneous. By applying Theorem
12.1
, we have:

\displaystyle\int_{S^{d-1}}\|\theta\|_{p}^{p}\ \lambda(\mathrm{d}\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\Big(\frac{d}{2}\Big)}{\Gamma\Big(\frac{d+p}{2}\Big)}\mathbb{E}[\|X\|_{p}^{p}]\text{ with $X_{i}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{2})$}

(12.124)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\Big(\frac{d}{2}\Big)}{\Gamma\Big(\frac{d+p}{2}\Big)}d\ \mathbb{E}[|X_{1}|_{p}^{p}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\Big(\frac{d}{2}\Big)}{\Gamma\Big(\frac{d+p}{2}\Big)}d\ \int|t|^{p}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-t^{2}}\mathrm{d}t.

On the other hand, let
\tilde{f}:\theta\mapsto|\langle\mathrm{diag}(\theta),S\rangle_{F}|^{p}
, then
\tilde{f}(\alpha\theta)=\alpha^{p}\tilde{f}(\theta)
and
\tilde{f}
is p-homogeneous. By applying Theorem
12.1
, we have:

\displaystyle\int_{S^{d-1}}|\langle\mathrm{diag}(\theta),S\rangle_{F}|^{p}\ \lambda(\mathrm{d}\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\Big(\frac{d}{2}\Big)}{\Gamma\Big(\frac{d+p}{2}\Big)}\mathbb{E}[|\langle\mathrm{diag}(X),S\rangle_{F}|^{p}]\text{ with $X_{i}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{2})$}

(12.125)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\Big(\frac{d}{2}\Big)}{\Gamma\Big(\frac{d+p}{2}\Big)}\int|t|^{p}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}S_{ii}^{2}\pi}}e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{\sum_{i}z_{ii}^{2}}}\ \mathrm{d}t\text{ as $\langle\mathrm{diag}(X),S\rangle_{F}=\sum_{i}S_{ii}X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\Big(0,\frac{\sum_{i}S_{ii}^{2}}{2}\Big)$}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\Big(\frac{d}{2}\Big)}{\Gamma\Big(\frac{d+p}{2}\Big)}\left(\sum_{i}S_{ii}^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\int|u|^{p}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}S_{ii}^{2}\pi}}e^{-u^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i}S_{ii}^{2}}\mathrm{d}u\text{ by $u=\frac{t}{\sqrt{\sum_{i}S_{ii}^{2}}}$}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\Big(\frac{d}{2}\Big)}{\Gamma\Big(\frac{d+p}{2}\Big)}\left(\sum_{i}S_{ii}^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\int|u|^{p}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-u^{2}}\mathrm{d}u.

Hence, we deduce that

\int_{S^{d-1}}|\langle\mathrm{diag}(\theta),S\rangle_{F}|^{p}\ \lambda(\mathrm{d}\theta)=\frac{1}{d}\left(\sum_{i}S_{ii}^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\int_{S^{d-1}}\|\theta\|_{p}^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(\theta).

(12.126)

∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.3
.

First, we show the upper bound of
\mathrm{SPDSW}_{p}
. Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})
and
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
an optimal coupling. Then, following the proof of
Bonnotte 2013
, and using
Lemma

12.1
combined with the fact that
(P^{A}\otimes P^{A})_{\#}\gamma\in\Pi(P^{A}_{\#}\mu,P^{A}_{\#}\nu)
for any
A\in S_{d}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\|A\|_{F}=1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{SPDSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{d}(\mathbb{R})}W_{p}^{p}(P^{A}_{\#}\mu,P^{A}_{\#}\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda_{S}(A)

(12.127)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{S_{d}(\mathbb{R})}\int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})\times S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}|P^{A}(X)-P^{A}(Y)|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(X,Y)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda_{S}(A)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{d}(\mathbb{R})}\int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})\times S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}|\langle A,\log X-\log Y\rangle_{F}|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(X,Y)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda_{S}(A)

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{d-1}}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{d}}\int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})\times S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}|\langle P\mathrm{diag}(\theta)P^{T},\log X-\log Y\rangle_{F}|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(X,Y)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda_{O}(P)\mathrm{d}\lambda(\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{d-1}}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{d}}\int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})\times S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}|\langle\mathrm{diag}(\theta),P^{T}(\log X-\log Y)P\rangle_{F}|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(X,Y)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda_{O}(P)\mathrm{d}\lambda(\theta).

By Lemma
12.8
, noting
S=P^{T}(\log X-\log Y)P
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{S^{d-1}}|\langle\mathrm{diag}(\theta),S\rangle_{F}|^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\left(\sum_{i}S_{ii}^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\int_{S^{d-1}}\|\theta\|_{p}^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(\theta)

(12.128)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{d}\|S\|_{F}^{p}\int_{S^{d-1}}\|\theta\|_{p}^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(\theta),

since
\|S\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{i,j}S_{ij}^{2}\geq\sum_{i}S_{ii}^{2}
. Moreover,
\|S\|_{F}=\|P^{T}(\log X-\log Y)P\|_{F}=\|\log X-\log Y\|_{F}
. Hence, coming back to (
12.127
), we find

\displaystyle\mathrm{SPDSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{d}\int_{S^{d-1}}\|\theta\|_{p}^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(\theta)\int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})\times S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}\|\log X-\log Y\|_{F}^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(X,Y)

(12.129)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d}\int_{S^{d-1}}\|\theta\|_{p}^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(\theta)\ W_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=c_{d,p}^{p}W_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu).

since
\gamma
is an optimal coupling between
\mu
and
\nu
for the Wasserstein distance with Log-Euclidean cost.

For the lower bound, let us first observe that

\displaystyle W_{1}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})\times S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}d_{LE}(X,Y)\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(X,Y)

(12.130)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int_{S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})\times S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})}\|\log X-\log Y\|_{F}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\ \int_{S_{d}(\mathbb{R})\times S_{d}(\mathbb{R})}\|U-V\|_{F}\ \mathrm{d}(\log\otimes\log)_{\#}\gamma(U,V)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\log_{\#}\mu,\log_{\#}\nu)}\ \int_{S_{d}(\mathbb{R})\times S_{d}(\mathbb{R})}\|U-V\|_{F}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(U,V)

\displaystyle=W_{1}(\log_{\#}\mu,\log_{\#}\nu),

where we used
Lemma

12.1
. Here, note that
W_{1}
must be understood with the groundcost metric which makes sense given the space,
i.e.

d_{LE}
for
S_{d}^{++}(\mathbb{R})
and
\|\cdot\|_{F}
for
S_{d}(\mathbb{R})
.

Using
Proposition

5.4
, we have

\mathrm{SymSW}_{1}(\log_{\#}\mu,\log_{\#}\nu)=\mathrm{SPDSW}_{1}(\mu,\nu).

(12.131)

Therefore, as
S_{d}(\mathbb{R})
is an Euclidean space of dimension
d(d+1)/2
, we can use
(
Bonnotte 2013
, Lemma 5.1.4)
and we obtain that

W_{1}(\log_{\#}\mu,\log_{\#}\nu)\leq C_{d(d+1)/2}R^{d(d+1)/(d(d+1)+2)}\mathrm{SymSW}_{1}(\log_{\#}\mu,\log_{\#}\nu)^{2/(d(d+1)+2)}.

(12.132)

Then, using that
\mathrm{SymSW}_{1}(\log_{\#}\mu,\log_{\#}\nu)=\mathrm{SPDSW}_{1}(\mu,\nu)
and
W_{1}(\log_{\#}\mu,\log_{\#}\nu)=W_{1}(\mu,\nu)
, we obtain

W_{1}(\mu,\nu)\leq C_{d(d+1)/2}R^{d(d+1)/(d(d+1)+2)}\mathrm{SPDSW}_{1}(\mu,\nu)^{2/(d(d+1)+2)}.

(12.133)

Now, following the proof of
Bonnotte 2013
, we use that on one hand,
W_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)\leq(2R)^{p-1}W_{1}(\mu,\nu)
, and on the other hand, by Hölder,
\mathrm{SPDSW}_{1}(\mu,\nu)\leq\mathrm{SPDSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)
. Hence, using inequalities (
12.129
) and (
12.133
), we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{SPDSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle\leq c_{d,p}^{p}W_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)

(12.134)

\displaystyle\leq(2R)^{p-1}W_{1}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}C_{d(d+1)/2}R^{p-1+d(d+1)/(d(d+1)+2)}\mathrm{SPDSW}_{1}(\mu,\nu)^{2/(d(d+1)/2)}

\displaystyle=C_{d,p}^{d}R^{p-2/(d(d+1))}\mathrm{SPDSW}_{1}(\mu,\nu)^{2/(d(d+1)+2)}.

∎

Complementary experiments

Brain Age Prediction

Performance of SPDSW-based brain age regression on 10-folds cross validation for one random seed.

In
Figure

12.1
, we display the Mean Absolute Error (MAE) and the
R^{2}
coefficient on 10-folds cross validation with one random seed.

\mathrm{SPDSW}
is run with time-frames of 2s and 1000 projections.

Figure 12.1:
Results of 10-folds cross validation on the Cam-CAN data-set for one random seed.
We display the Mean Absolute Error (MAE) and the
R^{2}
coefficient.

\mathrm{SPDSW}
, with time-frames of 2s and 1000 projections, performs best.
Note that Kernel Ridge regression based on the Log-Euclidean distance performs better than Ridge regression.

Performance of SPDSW-based brain age regression depending on number of projections.

In
Figure

12.2
, we display the MAE and
R^{2}
score on brain age regression with different numbers of projections for 10 random seeds.
In this example, the variance and scores are acceptable for 500 projections and more.

Figure 12.2:
Average results for 10 random seeds with 200, 500 and 1000 projections for
\mathrm{SPDSW}
compared to average MAE and
R^{2}
obtained with Ridge and Kernel Ridge regression on features from covariance estimates
(
Sabbagh et al. 2019
)
. With enough projections,
\mathrm{SPDSW}
kernel does not suffer from variance and performs best.

Performance of SPDSW-based brain age regression depending on timeframe length.

In
Figure

12.3
, we display the MAE and
R^{2}
score on brain age regression with different time-frame lengths for 10 random seeds.
The performance of
\mathrm{SPDSW}
-kernel Ridge regression depends on a trade-off between the number of samples in each distribution (smaller time-frames for more samples), and the level of noise in the covariance estimate (larger time-frame for less noise).
In this example, time-frames of
400
samples seems to be a good choice.

Figure 12.3:
Average MAE and
R^{2}
score on brain age regression with different time-frame lengths for 10 random seeds
The performance depends on the time-frame length, and there is a trade-off to find between number of samples and noise in the samples.

Domain Adaptation for BCI

Alignement.

We plot on
Figure

12.4
the classes of the target session (circles) and of the source session after alignment (crosses) on each subject. We observe that the classes seem to be well aligned, which explains why simple transformations work on this data-set. Hence, minimizing a discrepancy allows to align the classes even without taking them into account in the loss. More complicated data-sets might require taking into account the classes for the alignment.

Figure 12.4:
PCA representation on BCI data. Circles represent points from the target session and crosses points from the source after alignment.

Illustrations

Sample Complexity.

We illustrate
Proposition

3.13
in the particular case of
\mathrm{SPDSW}
in

12.6a
by plotting
\mathrm{SPDSW}
and the Wasserstein distance with Log-Euclidean ground cost (LEW) between samples drawn from the same Wishart distribution, for
d=2
and
d=50
.
\mathrm{SPDSW}
is computed with
L=1000
projections. We observe that
\mathrm{SPDSW}
converges with the same speed in both dimensions while LEW converges slower in dimension 50.

Projection Complexity.

We illustrate
Proposition

3.14
in the particular case of
\mathrm{SPDSW}
in
Figure

12.6b
by plotting the absolute error between
\widehat{\mathrm{SPDSW}}_{2,L}^{2}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{SPDSW}}_{2,L^{*}}^{2}
. We fix
L^{*}
at 10000 which gives a good idea of the true value of
\mathrm{SPDSW}
and we vary
L
between
1
and
10^{3}
evenly in log scale. We average the results over 100 runs and plot 95% confidence intervals. We observe that the Monte-Carlo error converges to 0 with a convergence rate of
O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}})
.

D=\mathrm{SPDSW}
and
D=\mathrm{LEW}
for

and

.

\mathrm{SPDSW}
and the
\mathrm{LogSW}
for

and

.
