Sample and projection complexity. Experiments are replicated 100 times and we report the 95% confidence intervals. We note
\hat{\mu}_{n}
and
\hat{\mu}^{\prime}_{n}
two different empirical distributions of
\mu
. The sample complexity of
\mathrm{SPDSW}
does not depend on the dimension contrary to Wasserstein. The projections complexity has a slope which decreases in
O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}})
.

12.3.3
Experimental details

Runtime

In
Figure

5.2
, we plot the runtime
w.r.t
the number of samples for different OT discrepancies. Namely, we compare
\mathrm{SPDSW}
,
\mathrm{HSPDSW}
,
\mathrm{LogSW}
, the Wasserstein distance with Affine-Invariant ground cost, the Wasserstein distance with Log-Euclidean ground cost, and the Sinkhorn algorithm used to compute the entropic regularized OT problem with Log-Euclidean ground cost. The distance ground costs are computed with
geoopt

(
Kochurov et al. 2020
)
while Wasserstein and Sinkhorn are computed with
POT

(
Flamary et al. 2021
)
. All computations are done on a A6000 GPU. We average the results over 20 runs and for
n\in\{100,215,464,1000,2154,4641,10000,21544,46415,100000\}
samples, which are evenly spaced in log scale, from a Wishart distribution in dimension
d=20
. For the sliced methods, we fix
L=200
projections. For the Sinkhorn algorithm, we use a stopping threshold of
10^{-10}
with maximum
10^{5}
iterations and a regularization parameter of
\epsilon=1
.

Brain Age Prediction

We reuse the code for preprocessing steps and benchmarking procedure described in
Engemann et al. 2022
for the CamCAN data-set, and available at
https://github.com/meeg-ml-benchmarks/brain-age-benchmark-paper
, which we recall here.

The data consist of measurements from 102 magnetometers and 204 gradiometers.
First, we apply a band-pass filtering between 0.1Hz and 49Hz.
Then, the signal is subsampled with a decimation factor of 5, leading to a sample frequency of 200Hz.
Then, we apply the temporal signal-space-separation (tSSS).
Default settings were applied for the harmonic decomposition (8 components of the internal sources, 3 for the external sources) on a 10-s sliding window.
To discard segments for which inner and outer signal components were poorly distinguishable, we applied a correlation threshold of 98%.

For analysis, the band frequencies used are the following: (0.1Hz, 1Hz), (1Hz, 4Hz), (4Hz, 8Hz), (8Hz, 15Hz), (15Hz, 26Hz), (26Hz, 35Hz), (35Hz, 49Hz).
The rank of the covariance matrices obtained after OAS is reduced to 53 with a PCA, which leads to the best score on this problem as mentioned in
Sabbagh et al. 2020
.

The code for the MEG experiments is essentially based on the work by
Engemann et al. 2022
, the class
SPDSW
available in the supplementary material, and the Kernel Ridge Regression of
scikit-learn
.
The full version will be added later in order to respect anonymity.

Domain Adaptation for BCI

For both the optimization over particles and over transformations, we use
geoopt

(
Kochurov et al. 2020
)
with the Riemannian gradient descent. We now detail the hyperparameters and the procedure.

First, the data from the BCI Competition IV 2a are preprocessed using the code from
Hersche et al. 2018
available at
https://github.com/MultiScale-BCI/IV-2a
. We applied a band-pass filter between 8 and 30 Hz. With these hyper-parameters, we get one regularized covariance matrix per subject.

For all experiments, we report the results averaged over 5 runs. For the sliced discrepancies, we always use
L=500
projections which we draw only once a the beginning. When optimizing over particles, we used a learning rate of
1000
for the sliced methods and of
10
for Wasserstein and Sinkhorn. The number of epochs was fixed at 500 for the cross-session task and for the cross-subject tasks. For the basic transformations, we always use 500 epochs and we choose a learning rate of
1e^{-1}
on cross session and
5e^{-1}
on cross subject for sliced methods, and of
1e^{-2}
for Wasserstein and Sinkhorn. For the Sinkhorn algorithm, we use
\epsilon=10
with the default hyperparameters from the
POT
implementation. Moreover, we only use one translation and rotation for the transformation.

Furthermore, the results reported for AISOTDA in
Table

5.1
and
Table

12.1
are taken from
Yair et al. 2019
(Table 1.a, column Alg.1 (u)). We note however that they may not have used the same preprocessing and hyperparameters to load the covariance matrices.

12.4
Appendix of
Chapter

6

12.4.1
Proofs of
Section

6.2

Proof of
Proposition

6.1

Proof of
Proposition

6.1
.

Optimal
\alpha
.
Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(S^{1})
,
\nu=\mathrm{Unif}(S^{1})
. Since
\nu
is the uniform distribution on
S^{1}
, its cdf is the identity on
[0,1]
(where we identified
S^{1}
and
[0,1]
). We can extend the cdf
F
on the real line as in
(
Rabin et al. 2011
)
with the convention
F(y+1)=F(y)+1
. Therefore,
F_{\nu}=\mathrm{Id}
on
\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, we know that for all
x\in S^{1}
,
(F_{\nu}-\alpha)^{-1}(x)=F_{\nu}^{-1}(x+\alpha)=x+\alpha
and

W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)=\inf_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\ \int_{0}^{1}|F_{\mu}^{-1}(t)-(F_{\nu}-\alpha)^{-1}(t)|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t.

(12.135)

For all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, let
f(\alpha)=\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\mu}^{-1}(t)-(F_{\nu}-\alpha)^{-1}(t)\big)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t
. Then, we have:

\displaystyle\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{R},\ f(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\mu}^{-1}(t)-t-\alpha\big)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t

(12.136)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\mu}^{-1}(t)-t\big)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t+\alpha^{2}-2\alpha\int_{0}^{1}(F_{\mu}^{-1}(t)-t)\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\mu}^{-1}(t)-t\big)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t+\alpha^{2}-2\alpha\left(\int_{0}^{1}x\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)-\frac{1}{2}\right),

where we used that
(F_{\mu}^{-1})_{\#}\mathrm{Unif}([0,1])=\mu
.

Hence,
f^{\prime}(\alpha)=0\iff\alpha=\int_{0}^{1}x\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)-\frac{1}{2}
.

Closed-form for empirical distributions.
Let
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in[0,1[^{n}
such that
x_{1}<\dots<x_{n}
and let
\mu_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
a discrete distribution.

To compute the closed-form of
W_{2}
between
\mu_{n}
and
\nu=\mathrm{Unif}(S^{1})
, we first have that the optimal
\alpha
is
\alpha_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}-\frac{1}{2}
. Moreover, we also have:

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{n},\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\mu_{n}}^{-1}(t)-(t+\hat{\alpha}_{n})\big)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t

(12.137)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}F_{\mu_{n}}^{-1}(t)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t-2\int_{0}^{1}tF_{\mu_{n}}^{-1}(t)\mathrm{d}t-2\hat{\alpha}_{n}\int_{0}^{1}F_{\mu_{n}}^{-1}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{3}+\hat{\alpha}_{n}+\hat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}.

Then, by noticing that
F_{\mu_{n}}^{-1}(t)=x_{i}
for all
t\in[F(x_{i}),F(x_{i+1})[
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}tF_{\mu_{n}}^{-1}(t)\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\frac{i-1}{n}}^{\frac{i}{n}}tx_{i}\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}(2i-1),

(12.138)

\int_{0}^{1}F_{\mu}^{-1}(t)^{2}\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2},\quad\int_{0}^{1}F_{\mu}^{-1}(t)\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i},

(12.139)

and we also have:

\hat{\alpha}_{n}+\hat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}-\frac{1}{2}+\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\Big)^{2}+\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\Big)^{2}-\frac{1}{4}.

(12.140)

Then, by plugging these results into (
12.137
), we obtain

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{n},\nu)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(2i-1)x_{i}-2\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\Big)^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}+\frac{1}{3}+\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\Big)^{2}-\frac{1}{4}

(12.141)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}-\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\Big)^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(n+1-2i)x_{i}+\frac{1}{12}.

∎

Proof of the closed-form (
6.10
)

Here, we show the equality derived in (
6.10
), which we recall:

\forall U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2},\forall x\in S^{d-1},\ P^{U}(x)=U^{T}\argmin_{y\in\mathrm{span}(UU^{T})\cap S^{d-1}}\ d_{S^{d-1}}(x,y)=\argmin_{z\in S^{1}}\ d_{S^{d-1}}(x,Uz).

(12.142)

Proof.

Let
U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2}
. Then the great circle generated by
U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2}
is defined as the intersection between
\mathrm{span}(UU^{T})
and
S^{d-1}
. And we have the following characterization:

\displaystyle x\in\mathrm{span}(UU^{T})\cap S^{d-1}

\displaystyle\iff\exists y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ x=UU^{T}y\ \text{and}\ \|x\|_{2}^{2}=1

\displaystyle\iff\exists y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ x=UU^{T}y\ \text{and}\ \|UU^{T}y\|_{2}^{2}=y^{T}UU^{T}y=\|U^{T}y\|_{2}^{2}=1

\displaystyle\iff\exists z\in S^{1},\ x=Uz.

And we deduce that

\forall U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2},x\in S^{d-1},\ P^{U}(x)=\argmin_{z\in S^{1}}\ d_{S^{d-1}}(x,Uz).

(12.143)

∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.1

Proof of
Lemma

6.1
.

Let
U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2}
and
x\in S^{d-1}
such that
U^{T}x\neq 0
. Denote
U=(u_{1}\ u_{2})
,
i.e.
the
2
-plane
E
is
E=\mathrm{span}(UU^{T})=\mathrm{span}(u_{1},u_{2})
and
(u_{1},u_{2})
is an orthonormal basis of
E
. Then, for all
x\in S^{d-1}
, the projection on
E
is
p^{E}(x)=\langle u_{1},x\rangle u_{1}+\langle u_{2},x\rangle u_{2}=UU^{T}x
.

Now, let us compute the geodesic distance between
x\in S^{d-1}
and
\frac{p^{E}(x)}{\|p^{E}(x)\|_{2}}\in E\cap S^{d-1}
:

d_{S^{d-1}}\left(x,\frac{p^{E}(x)}{\|p^{E}(x)\|_{2}}\right)=\arccos\left(\langle x,\frac{p^{E}(x)}{\|p^{E}(x)\|_{2}}\rangle\right)=\arccos(\|p^{E}(x)\|_{2}),

(12.144)

using that
x=p^{E}(x)+p^{E^{\bot}}(x)
.

Let
y\in E\cap S^{d-1}
another point on the great circle. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

\langle x,y\rangle=\langle p^{E}(x),y\rangle\leq\|p^{E}(x)\|_{2}\|y\|_{2}=\|p^{E}(x)\|_{2}.

(12.145)

Therefore, using that
\arccos
is decreasing on
(-1,1)
,

d_{S^{d-1}}(x,y)=\arccos(\langle x,y\rangle)\geq\arccos(\|p^{E}(x)\|_{2})=d_{S^{d-1}}\left(x,\frac{p^{E}(x)}{\|p^{E}(x)\|_{2}}\right).

(12.146)

Moreover, we have equality if and only if
y=\lambda p^{E}(x)
. And since
y\in S^{d-1}
,
|\lambda|=\frac{1}{\|p^{E}(x)\|_{2}}
. Using again that
\arccos
is decreasing, we deduce that the minimum is well attained in
y=\frac{p^{E}(x)}{\|p^{E}(x)\|_{2}}=\frac{UU^{T}x}{\|UU^{T}x\|_{2}}
.

Finally, using that
\|UU^{T}x\|_{2}=x^{T}UU^{T}UU^{T}x=x^{T}UU^{T}x=\|U^{T}x\|_{2}
, we deduce that

P^{U}(x)=\frac{U^{T}x}{\|U^{T}x\|_{2}}.

(12.147)

Finally, by noticing that the projection is unique if and only if
U^{T}x=0
, and using
(
Bardelli and Mennucci 2017
, Proposition 4.2)
which states that there is a unique projection for a.e.
x
, we deduce that
\{x\in S^{d-1},\ U^{T}x=0\}
is of measure null and hence, for a.e.
x\in S^{d-1}
, we have the result.
∎

12.4.2
Proofs of
Section

6.3

Proof of
Proposition

6.2

Proof of
Proposition

6.2
.

Let
p\geq 1
.
First, it is straightforward to see that for all
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(S^{d-1})
,
\mathrm{SSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)\geq 0
,
\mathrm{SSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)=\mathrm{SSW}_{p}(\nu,\mu)
,
\mu=\nu\implies\mathrm{SSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)=0
and that we have the triangular inequality since

\displaystyle\forall\mu,\nu,\alpha\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(S^{d-1}),\ \mathrm{SSW}_{p}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\Big(\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}W_{p}^{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\mu,P^{U}_{\#}\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}

(12.148)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\big(W_{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\mu,P^{U}_{\#}\alpha)+W_{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\alpha,P^{U}_{\#}\nu)\big)^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}W_{p}^{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\mu,P^{U}_{\#}\alpha)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle+\Big(\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}W_{p}^{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\alpha,P^{U}_{\#}\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{SSW}_{p}(\mu,\alpha)+\mathrm{SSW}_{p}(\alpha,\nu),

using the triangular inequality for
W_{p}
and the Minkowski inequality.
Therefore, it is at least a pseudo-distance.

∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.3

Proof of
Proposition

6.3
.

Let
U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2}
,
z\in S^{1}
. Denote
E=\mathrm{span}(UU^{T})
the 2-plane generating the great circle, and
E^{\bot}
its orthogonal complementary. Hence,
E\oplus E^{\bot}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathrm{dim}(E^{\bot})=d-2
. Now, let
F=E^{\bot}\oplus\mathrm{span}(Uz)
. Since
Uz=UU^{T}Uz\in E
, we have that
\mathrm{dim}(F)=d-1
. Hence,
F
is a hyperplane and
F\cap S^{d-1}
is a
(d-2)
-dimensional subsphere of
S^{d-1}
.

Now, for the first inclusion, let
x\in\{x\in S^{d-1},\ P^{U}(x)=z\}
. First, we show that
x\in F\cap S^{d-1}
. By Lemma
6.1
and hypothesis, we know that
P^{U}(x)=\frac{U^{T}x}{\|U^{T}x\|_{2}}=z
. By denoting by
p^{E}
the projection on
E
, we have:

p^{E}(x)=UU^{T}x=U(\|U^{T}x\|_{2}z)=\|U^{T}x\|_{2}Uz\in\mathrm{span}(Uz).

(12.149)

Hence,
x=p^{E}(x)+x_{E^{\bot}}=\|U^{T}x\|_{2}Uz+x_{E^{\bot}}\in F
. Moreover, as

\langle x,Uz\rangle=\|U^{T}x\|_{2}\langle Uz,Uz\rangle=\|U^{T}x\|_{2}>0,

(12.150)

we deduce that
x\in\{F\cap S^{d-1},\ \langle x,Uz\rangle>0\}
.

For the other inclusion, let
x\in\{F\cap S^{d-1},\ \langle x,Uz\rangle>0\}
. Since
x\in F
, we have
x=x_{E^{\bot}}+\lambda Uz
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence, using Lemma
6.1
,

P^{U}(x)=\frac{U^{T}x}{\|U^{T}x\|_{2}}=\frac{\lambda}{|\lambda|}\frac{z}{\|z\|_{2}}=\mathrm{sign}(\lambda)z.

(12.151)

But, we also have
\langle x,Uz\rangle=\lambda\|Uz\|_{2}^{2}=\lambda>0
. Therefore,
\mathrm{sign}(\lambda)=1
and
P^{U}(x)=z
.

Finally, we conclude that
\{x\in S^{d-1},\ P^{U}(x)=z\rangle\}=\{x\in F\cap S^{d-1},\ \langle x,Uz\rangle>0\}
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.4

Proof of
Proposition

6.4
.

Let
f\in L^{1}(S^{d-1})
,
g\in C_{b}(S^{1}\times\mathbb{V}_{d,2})
, and denote
\tilde{f}
such that for all
y\in S^{d-1}
,
\tilde{f}(y)=f(O_{U}^{T}y)
with
O_{U}\in SO(d)
the rotation defined such that for any
z\in S^{1}
,
O_{U}Uz\in\mathrm{span}(e_{d-1},e_{d})
. Also, note that
P^{U_{0}}(O_{U}x)=\frac{U_{0}^{T}O_{U}x}{\|U_{0}^{T}O_{U}x\|_{2}}=\frac{U^{T}x}{\|U^{T}x\|_{2}}=P^{U}(x)
. Then,

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{R}f,g\rangle_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}

(12.152)

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{1}}\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\tilde{R}f(z,U)g(z,U)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\mathrm{d}\sigma_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{1}}\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\tilde{R}\tilde{f}_{U}(z,U_{0})g(z,U)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\mathrm{d}\sigma_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\tilde{R}\tilde{f}_{U}((\cos\theta_{d-1},\sin\theta_{d-1}),U_{0})g((\cos\theta_{d-1},\sin\theta_{d-1}),U)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\mathrm{d}\theta_{d-1}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\int_{[0,\pi]^{d-2}}\tilde{f}_{U}(\varphi(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{d-1}))g((\cos\theta_{d-1},\sin\theta_{d-1}),U)\prod_{i=1}^{d-2}\sin(\theta_{i})^{d-i-1}\ \mathrm{d}\theta_{1}\dots\mathrm{d}\theta_{d-2}\mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\mathrm{d}\theta_{d-1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\int_{S^{d-1}}\tilde{f}_{U}(y)g(P^{U_{0}}(y),U)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma_{d}(y)\mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\quad\text{ using $y=\varphi(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{d-1})$}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\int_{S^{d-1}}f(O_{U}^{T}y)g(P^{U_{0}}(y),U)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma_{d}(y)\mathrm{d}\sigma(U)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\int_{S^{d-1}}f(x)g(P^{U_{0}}(O_{U}x),U)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma_{d}(x)\mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\quad\text{using $x=O_{U}^{T}y$ and rotational invariance of $\sigma_{d}$}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\int_{S^{d-1}}f(x)g(P^{U}(x),U)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma_{d}(x)\mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\quad\text{using that $U=O_{U}^{T}U_{0}$}

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{d-1}}f(x)\tilde{R}^{*}g(x)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma_{d}(x)

\displaystyle=\langle f,\tilde{R}^{*}g\rangle_{S^{d-1}}.

∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.5

Proof of
Proposition

6.5
.

Let
g\in C_{b}(S^{1}\times\mathbb{V}_{d,2})
,b y applying the Fubini theorem,

\displaystyle\ \int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\int_{S^{1}}g(z,U)\ (\tilde{R}\mu)^{U}(\mathrm{d}z)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}g(z,U)\ \mathrm{d}(\tilde{R}\mu)(z,U)

(12.153)

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{d-1}}\tilde{R}^{*}g(x)\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}g(P^{U}(x),U)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\int_{S^{d-1}}g(P^{U}(x),U)\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\sigma(U)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\int_{S^{1}}g(z,U)\ \mathrm{d}(P^{U}_{\#}\mu)(z)\mathrm{d}\sigma(U).

Hence, for
\sigma
-almost every
U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2}
,
(\tilde{R}\mu)^{U}=P^{U}_{\#}\mu
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.6

Proof of
Proposition

6.6
.

Let
f\in L^{1}(S^{d-1})
,
z\in S^{1}
,
U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2}
, then by
Proposition

6.3
,

\displaystyle\tilde{R}f(z,U)

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{d-1}\cap F}f(x)\mathbb{1}_{\{\langle x,Uz\rangle>0\}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(x).

(12.154)

F
is a hyperplane. Let
O\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be the rotation such that for all
x\in F
,
Ox\in\mathrm{span}(e_{1},\dots,e_{d-1})=\tilde{F}
where
(e_{1},\dots,e_{d})
is the canonical basis. By applying the change of variable
Ox=y
, and since the surface measure is rotationally invariant, we obtain

\tilde{R}f(z,U)=\int_{O(F\cap S^{d-1})}f(O^{T}y)\mathbb{1}_{\{\langle O^{T}y,Uz\rangle>0\}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(y)=\int_{\tilde{F}\cap S^{d-1}}f(O^{T}y)\mathbb{1}_{\{\langle y,OUz\rangle>0\}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(y).

(12.155)

Now, we have that
OU\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2}
since
(OU)^{T}(OU)=I_{2}
, and since
Uz\in F
,
OUz\in\tilde{F}
. For all
y\in\tilde{F}
, we have
\langle y,e_{d}\rangle=y_{d}=0
. Let
J=\begin{pmatrix}I_{d-1}\\
0_{1,d-1}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-1)}
, then for all
y\in\tilde{F}\cap S^{d-1}
,
y=J\tilde{y}
where
\tilde{y}\in S^{d-2}
is composed of the
d-1
first coordinates of
y
.

Let’s define, for all
\tilde{y}\in S^{d-2}
,
\tilde{f}(\tilde{y})=f(O^{T}J\tilde{y})
,
\tilde{U}=J^{T}OU
.

Then, since
\tilde{F}\cap S^{d-1}\cong S^{d-2}
, we can write:

\tilde{R}f(z,U)=\int_{S^{d-2}}\tilde{f}(\tilde{y})\mathbb{1}_{\{\langle\tilde{y},\tilde{U}z\rangle>0\}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(\tilde{y})=\mathcal{H}_{d-2}\tilde{f}(\tilde{U}z).

(12.156)

∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.7

First, we recall Lemma 2.3 of
(
Rubin 1999
)
on
S^{d-2}
. In the following, we omit the indices for
\mathcal{H}
which is always on
S^{d-2}
. Note that for
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(S^{d-2})
,
\mathcal{H}\mu(x)=\mu\big(\{x\in S^{d-2},\ \langle x,y\rangle\geq 0\}\big)
and for
f\in L^{1}(S^{d-2})
,
\langle\mathcal{H}\mu,f\rangle=\langle\mu,\mathcal{H}f\rangle
.

Lemma 12.9

(Lemma 2.3
(
Rubin 1999
)
)
.

\mathrm{ker}(\mathcal{H})=\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{even}}(S^{d-2}),\ \mu(S^{d-2})=0\}
where
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{even}}
is the set of even measures,
i.e.
measures such that for all
f\in C(S^{d-2})
,
\langle\mu,f\rangle=\langle\mu,f^{-}\rangle
where
f^{-}(x)=f(-x)
for all
x\in S^{d-2}
.

Proof of
Proposition

6.7
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{ac}(S^{d-1})
. First, we notice that the density of
\tilde{R}\mu

w.r.t.

\lambda\otimes\sigma
is, for all
z\in S^{1}
,
U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2}
,

(\tilde{R}\mu)(z,U)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S^{d-1}}\mathbb{1}_{\{P^{U}(x)=z\}}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{F\cap S^{d-1}}\mathbb{1}_{\{\langle x,Uz\rangle>0\}}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x).

(12.157)

Indeed, using
Proposition

6.4
, and
Proposition

6.3
, we have for all
g\in C_{b}(S^{1}\times\mathbb{V}_{d,2})
,

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{R}\mu,g\rangle_{S^{1}\times\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}=\langle\mu,\tilde{R}^{*}g\rangle_{S^{d-1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{d-1}}\tilde{R}^{*}g(x)\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)

(12.158)

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}g(P^{U}(x),U)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S^{d-1}}\int_{S^{1}}\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}g(z,U)\mathbb{1}_{\{z=P^{U}(x)\}}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(z)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}\times S^{1}}g(z,U)\int_{S^{d-1}}\mathbb{1}_{\{z=P^{U}(x)\}}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\ \mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(z)\mathrm{d}\sigma(U)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}\times S^{1}}g(z,U)\int_{F\cap S^{d-1}}\mathbb{1}_{\{\langle x,Uz\rangle>0\}}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\ \mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(z)\mathrm{d}\sigma(U).

Hence, using
Proposition

6.6
, we can write
(\tilde{R}\mu)(z,U)=\frac{1}{2\pi}(\mathcal{H}\tilde{\mu})(\tilde{U}z)
where
\tilde{\mu}=J^{T}_{\#}O_{\#}\mu
.

Now, let
\mu\in\mathrm{ker}(\tilde{R})
, then for all
z\in S^{1}
,
U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2}
,
\tilde{R}\mu(z,U)=\mathcal{H}\tilde{\mu}(\tilde{U}z)=0
and hence
\tilde{\mu}\in\mathrm{ker}(\mathcal{H})=\{\tilde{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{even}}(S^{d-2}),\ \tilde{\mu}(S^{d-2})=0\}
.

First, let’s show that
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{even}}(S^{d-1})
. Let
f\in C(S^{d-1})
and
U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2}
, then, by using the same notation as in Propositions
6.3
and
6.6
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mu,f\rangle_{S^{d-1}}=\int_{S^{d-1}}f(x)\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S^{1}}\int_{S^{d-1}}f(x)\mathbb{1}_{\{z=P^{U}(x)\}}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(z)

(12.159)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S^{1}}\int_{F\cap S^{d-1}}f(x)\mathbb{1}_{\{\langle x,Uz\rangle>0\}}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(z)\quad\text{ by Prop. \ref{prop:integration_set_ssw}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S^{1}}\int_{S^{d-2}}\tilde{f}(y)\mathbb{1}_{\{\langle y,\tilde{U}z\rangle>0\}}\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mu}(y)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S^{1}}\langle\mathcal{H}\tilde{\mu}(\tilde{U}z),\tilde{f}\rangle_{S^{d-2}}\ \mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S^{1}}\langle\tilde{\mu},\mathcal{H}\tilde{f}(\tilde{U}z)\rangle_{S^{d-2}}\ \mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S^{1}}\langle\tilde{\mu},(\mathcal{H}\tilde{f})^{-}(\tilde{U}z)\rangle_{S^{d-2}}\ \mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}(z)\quad\text{ since $\tilde{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{even}}$}

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{d-1}}f^{-}(x)\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\langle\mu,f^{-}\rangle_{S^{d-1}},

using for the last line all the opposite transformations. Therefore,
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{even}}(S^{d-1})
.

Now, we need to find on which set the measure is null. We have

\displaystyle\forall z\in S^{1},U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2},\ \tilde{\mu}(S^{d-2})=0

(12.160)

\displaystyle\iff\forall z\in S^{1},U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2},\ \mu(O^{-1}((J^{T})^{-1}(S^{d-2})))=\mu(F\cap S^{d-1})=0.

Hence, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathrm{ker}(\tilde{R})=\{

\displaystyle\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{even}}(S^{d-1}),\ \forall U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2},\forall z\in S^{1},F=\mathrm{span}(UU^{T})^{\bot}\cap\mathrm{span}(Uz),

(12.161)

\displaystyle\mu(F\cap S^{d-1})=0\}.

Moreover, we have that
\cup_{U,z}F_{U,z}\cap S^{d-1}=\{H\cap S^{d-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d},\ \mathrm{dim}(H)=d-1\}
.

Indeed, on the one hand, let H an hyperplane,
x\in H\cap S^{d-1}
,
U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2}
, and note
z=P^{U}(x)
. Then,
x\in F\cap S^{d-1}
by
Proposition

6.3
and
H\cap S^{d-1}\subset\cup_{U,z}F_{U,z}
.

On the other hand, let
U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2}
,
z\in S^{1}
,
F
is a hyperplane since
\mathrm{dim}(F)=d-1
and therefore
F\cap S^{d-1}\subset\{H,\ \mathrm{dim}(H)=d-1\}
.

Finally, we deduce that

\mathrm{ker}(\tilde{R})=\big\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{even}}(S^{d-1}),\ \forall H\in\mathcal{G}_{d,d-1},\ \mu(H\cap S^{d-1})=0\big\}.

(12.162)

∎

12.4.3
Proofs of
Section

6.4

Proof of
Proposition

6.8

Proof of
Proposition

6.8
.

Since the Wasserstein distance metrizes the weak convergence (Corollary 6.11
(
Villani 2009
)
), we have
P^{U}_{\#}\mu_{k}\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}P^{U}_{\#}\mu
(by continuity)
\iff W_{p}^{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\mu_{k},P^{U}_{\#}\mu)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}0
and hence by the dominated convergence theorem,
\mathrm{SSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu_{k},\mu)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}0
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.9

Proof of
Proposition

6.9
.

By using the triangle inequality, Fubini-Tonelli, and the hypothesis on the sample complexity of
W_{p}^{p}
on
S^{1}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\mathrm{SSW}_{p}^{p}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{n})-\mathrm{SSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)|]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\big(W_{p}^{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\hat{\mu}_{n},P^{U}_{\#}\hat{\nu}_{n})-W_{p}^{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\mu,P^{U}_{\#}\nu)\big)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\right|\right]

(12.163)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\big|W_{p}^{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\hat{\mu}_{n},P^{U}_{\#}\hat{\nu}_{n})-W_{p}^{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\mu,P^{U}_{\#}\nu)\big|\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\big|W_{p}^{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\hat{\mu}_{n},P^{U}_{\#}\hat{\nu}_{n})-W_{p}^{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\mu,P^{U}_{\#}\nu)\big|\right]\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\beta(p,n)\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U)

\displaystyle=\beta(p,n).

∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.10

Proof of
Proposition

6.10
.

Let
(U_{i})_{i=1}^{L}
be iid samples of
\sigma
. Then, by first using Jensen inequality and then remembering that
\mathbb{E}_{U}[W_{p}^{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\mu,P^{U}_{\#}\nu)]=\mathrm{SSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{U}\left[|\widehat{\mathrm{SSW}}_{p,L}^{p}(\mu,\nu)-\mathrm{SSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)|\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{U}\left[\left|\widehat{\mathrm{SSW}}_{p,L}^{p}(\mu,\nu)-\mathrm{SSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)\right|^{2}\right]

(12.164)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{U}\left[\left|\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\big(W_{p}^{p}(P^{U_{i}}_{\#}\mu,P^{U_{i}}_{\#}\nu)-\mathrm{SSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)\big)\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L^{2}}\mathrm{Var}_{U}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{L}W_{p}^{p}(P^{U_{i}}_{\#}\mu,P^{U_{i}}_{\#}\nu)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\mathrm{Var}_{U}\left(W_{p}^{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\mu,P^{U}_{\#}\nu)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\int_{\mathbb{V}_{d,2}}\left(W_{p}^{p}(P^{U}_{\#}\mu,P^{U}_{\#}\nu)-\mathrm{SSW}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)\right)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\sigma(U).

∎

12.4.4
Background on the Sphere

Uniqueness of the Projection

Here, we discuss the uniqueness of the projection
P^{U}
for almost every
x
. For that, we recall some results of
(
Bardelli and Mennucci 2017
)
.

Let
M
be a closed subset of a complete finite-dimensional Riemannian manifold
N
. Let
d
be the Riemannian distance on
N
. Then, the distance from the set
M
is defined as

d_{M}(x)=\inf_{y\in M}\ d(x,y).

(12.165)

The infimum is a minimum since
M
is closed and
N
locally compact, but the minimum might not be unique. When it is unique, let’s denote the point which attains the minimum as
\pi(x)
,
i.e.

d(x,\pi(x))=d_{M}(x)
.

Proposition 12.1

(Proposition 4.2 in
(
Bardelli and Mennucci 2017
)
)
.

Let
M
be a closed set in a complete
m
-dimensional Riemannian manifold
N
. Then, for almost every
x
, there exists a unique point
\pi(x)\in M
that realizes the minimum of the distance from
x
.

From this Proposition, they further deduce that the measure
\pi_{\#}\gamma
is well defined on
M
with
\gamma
a locally absolutely continuous measure
w.r.t.
the Lebesgue measure.

In our setting, for all
U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2}
, we want to project a measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(S^{d-1})
on the great circle
\mathrm{span}(UU^{T})\cap S^{-1}
. Hence, we have
N=S^{d-1}
which is a complete finite-dimensional Riemannian manifold and
M=\mathrm{span}(UU^{T})\cap S^{d-1}
a closed set in
N
. Therefore, we can apply Proposition
12.1
and the push-forward measures are well defined for absolutely continuous measures.

Optimization on the Sphere

Let
F:S^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}
be some functional on the sphere. Then, we can perform a gradient descent on a Riemannian manifold by following the geodesics, which are the counterpart of straight lines in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Hence, the gradient descent algorithm
(
Absil et al. 2009
,
Bonnabel 2013
)
reads as

\forall k\geq 0,\ x_{k+1}=\exp_{x_{k}}\big(-\gamma\mathrm{grad}f(x)\big),

(12.166)

where for all
x\in S^{d-1}
,
\exp_{x}:T_{x}S^{d-1}\to S^{d-1}
is a map from the tangent space
T_{x}S^{d-1}=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ \langle x,v\rangle=0\}
to
S^{d-1}
such that for all
v\in T_{x}S^{d-1}
,
\exp_{x}(v)=\gamma_{v}(1)
with
\gamma_{v}
the unique geodesic starting from
x
with speed
v
,
\emph{i.e.}

\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma^{\prime}(0)=v
.

For
S^{d-1}
, the exponential map is known and is

\forall x\in S^{d-1},\forall v\in T_{x}S^{d-1},\ \exp_{x}(v)=\cos(\|v\|_{2})x+\sin(\|v\|_{2})\frac{v}{\|v\|_{2}}.

(12.167)

Moreover, the Riemannian gradient on
S^{d-1}
is known as
(
Absil et al. 2009
, Eq. 3.37)

\mathrm{grad}f(x)=\mathrm{Proj}_{x}(\nabla f(x))=\nabla f(x)-\langle\nabla f(x),x\rangle x,

(12.168)

\mathrm{Proj}_{x}
denoting the orthogonal projection on
T_{x}S^{d-1}
.

For more details, we refer to
(
Absil et al. 2009
,
Boumal 2023
)
.

Von Mises-Fisher Distribution

The von Mises-Fisher (vMF) distribution is a distribution on
S^{d-1}
characterized by a concentration parameter
\kappa>0
and a location parameter
\mu\in S^{d-1}
through the density

\forall\theta\in S^{d-1},\ f_{\mathrm{vMF}}(\theta;\mu,\kappa)=\frac{\kappa^{d/2-1}}{(2\pi)^{d/2}I_{d/2-1}(\kappa)}\exp(\kappa\mu^{T}\theta),

(12.169)

where
I_{\nu}(\kappa)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\exp(\kappa\cos(\theta))\cos(\nu\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta
is the modified Bessel function of the first kind.

Several algorithms allow to sample from it, see
e.g.

(
Wood 1994
,
Ulrich 1984
)
for algorithms using rejection sampling or
(
Kurz and Hanebeck 2015
)
without rejection sampling.

For
d=1
, the vMF coincides with the von Mises (vM) distribution, which has for density

\forall\theta\in[-\pi,\pi[,\ f_{\mathrm{vM}}(\theta;\mu,\kappa)=\frac{1}{I_{0}(\kappa)}\exp(\kappa\cos(\theta-\mu)),

(12.170)

with
\mu\in[0,2\pi[
the mean direction and
\kappa>0
its concentration parameter. We refer to
(
Mardia et al. 2000
, Section 3.5 and Chapter 9)
for more details on these distributions.

In particular, for
\kappa=0
, the vMF (resp. vM) distribution coincides with the uniform distribution on the sphere (resp. the circle).

Jung 2021
studied the law of the projection of a vMF on a great circle. In particular, they showed that, while the vMF plays the role of the normal distributions for directional data, the projection actually does not follow a von Mises distribution. More precisely, they showed the following theorem:

Theorem 12.2

(Theorem 3.1 in
(
Jung 2021
)
)
.

Let
d\geq 3
,
X\sim\mathrm{vMF}(\mu,\kappa)\in S^{d-1}
,
U\in\mathbb{V}_{d,2}
and
T=P^{U}(X)
the projection on the great circle generated by
U
. Then, the density function of
T
is

\forall t\in[-\pi,\pi[,\ f(t)=\int_{0}^{1}f_{R}(r)f_{\mathrm{vM}}(t;0,\kappa\cos(\delta)r)\ \mathrm{d}r,

(12.171)

where
\delta
is the deviation of the great circle (geodesic) from
\mu
and the mixing density is

\forall r\in]0,1[,\ f_{R}(r)=\frac{2}{I_{\nu}^{*}(\kappa)}I_{0}(\kappa\cos(\delta)r)r(1-r^{2})^{\nu-1}I_{\nu-1}^{*}(\kappa\sin(\delta)\sqrt{1-r^{2}}),

(12.172)

with
\nu=(d-2)/2
and
I_{\nu}^{*}(z)=(\frac{z}{2})^{-\nu}I_{\nu}(z)
for
z>0
,
I_{\nu}^{*}(0)=1/\Gamma(\nu+1)
.

Hence, as noticed by
Jung 2021
, in the particular case
\kappa=0
,
i.e.

X\sim\mathrm{Unif}(S^{d-1})
, then

f(t)=\int_{0}^{1}f_{R}(r)f_{\mathrm{vM}}(t;0,0)\ \mathrm{d}r=f_{\mathrm{vM}(t;0,0)}\int_{0}^{1}f_{R}(r)\mathrm{d}r=f_{\mathrm{vM}}(t;0,0),

(12.173)

and hence
T\sim\mathrm{Unif}(S^{1})
.

Normalizing Flows on the Sphere

Normalizing flows
(
Papamakarios et al. 2021
)
are invertible transformations. There has been a recent interest in defining such transformations on manifolds, and in particular on the sphere
(
Rezende et al. 2020
,
Cohen et al. 2021a
,
Rezende and Racanière 2021
)
.

Exponential map normalizing flows.

Here, we implemented the Exponential map normalizing flows introduced in
(
Rezende et al. 2020
)
. The transformation
T
is

\forall x\in S^{d-1},\ z=T(x)=\exp_{x}\big(\mathrm{Proj}_{x}(\nabla\phi(x))\big),

(12.174)

where
\phi(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\beta_{i}}e^{\beta_{i}(x^{T}\mu_{i}-1)}
,
\alpha_{i}\geq 0
,
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\leq 1
,
\mu_{i}\in S^{d-1}
and
\beta_{i}>0
for all
i
.
(\alpha_{i})_{i}
,
(\beta_{i})_{i}
and
(\mu_{i})_{i}
are the learnable parameters.

The density of
z
can be obtained as

p_{Z}(z)=p_{X}(x)\det\big(E(x)^{T}J_{T}(x)^{T}J_{T}(x)E(x)\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}},

(12.175)

where
J_{f}
is the Jacobian in the embedded space and
E(x)
it the matrix whose columns form an orthonormal basis of
T_{x}S^{d-1}
.

The common way of training normalizing flows is to use either the reverse or forward KL divergence. Here, we use them with a different loss, namely SSW.

Stereographic projection.

The stereographic projection
\rho:S^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
maps the sphere
S^{d-1}
to the Euclidean space. A strategy first introduced in
(
Gemici et al. 2016
)
is to use it before applying a normalizing flows in the Euclidean space in order to map some prior, and which allows to perform density estimation.

More precisely, the stereographic projection is defined as

\forall x\in S^{d-1},\ \rho(x)=\frac{x_{2:d}}{1+x_{1}},

(12.176)

and its inverse is

\forall u\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1},\ \rho^{-1}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}2\frac{u}{\|u\|_{2}^{2}+1}\\
1-\frac{2}{\|u\|_{2}^{2}+1}\end{pmatrix}.

(12.177)

Gemici et al. 2016
derived the change of variable formula for this transformation, which comes from the theory of probability between manifolds. If we have a transformation
T=f\circ\rho
, where
f
is a normalizing flows on
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
,
e.g.
a RealNVP
(
Dinh et al. 2017
)
, then the log density of the target distribution can be obtained as

\displaystyle\log p(x)

\displaystyle=\log p_{Z}(z)+\log|\det J_{f}(z)|-\frac{1}{2}\log|\det J_{\rho^{-1}}^{T}J_{\rho^{-1}}(\rho(x))|

(12.178)

\displaystyle=\log p_{Z}(z)+\log|\det J_{f}(z)|-d\log\left(\frac{2}{\|\rho(x)\|_{2}^{2}+1}\right),

where we used the formula of
(
Gemici et al. 2016
)
for the change of variable formula of
\rho
, and where
p_{Z}
is the density of some prior on
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
, typically of a standard Gaussian.
We refer to
(
Gemici et al. 2016
,
Mathieu and Nickel 2020
)
for more details about these transformations.

12.4.5
Details of the Experiments

Gradient Flows on Mixture of von Mises-Fisher Distributions

For the experiment in
Section

6.5.1
, we use as target distribution a mixture of 6 vMF distributions from which we have access to samples. We refer to
Section

12.4.4
for background on vMF distributions.

The 6 vMF distributions have weights
1/6
, concentration parameter
\kappa=10
and location parameters
\mu_{1}=(1,0,0)
,
\mu_{2}=(0,1,0)
,
\mu_{3}=(0,0,1)
,
\mu_{4}=(-1,0,0)
,
\mu_{5}=(0,-1,0)
and
\mu_{6}=(0,0,-1)
.

We approximate the distribution using the empirical distribution,
i.e.

\hat{\mu}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
and we optimize over the particles
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
. To optimize over particles, we can either use a projected gradient descent:

\begin{cases}x^{(k+1)}=x^{(k)}-\gamma\nabla_{x^{(k)}}SSW_{2}^{2}(\hat{\mu}_{k},\nu)\\
x^{(k+1)}=\frac{x^{(k+1)}}{\|x^{(k+1)}\|_{2}},\end{cases}

(12.179)

or a Riemannian gradient descent on the sphere
(
Absil et al. 2009
)
(see
Section

12.4.4
for more details). Note that the projected gradient descent is a Riemannian gradient descent with retraction
(
Boumal 2023
)
.

Earth data estimation

Let
T
be a normalizing flow (NF). For a density estimation task, we have access to a distribution
\mu
through samples
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
,
i.e.
through the empirical measure
\hat{\mu}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
. And the goal is to find an invertible transformation
T
such that
T_{\#}\mu=p_{Z}
, where
p_{Z}
is a prior distribution for which we know the density. In that case, indeed, the density of
\mu
, denoted as
f_{\mu}
can be obtained as

\forall x,\ f_{\mu}(x)=p_{Z}(T(x))|\det J_{T}(x)|.

(12.180)

For the invertible transform, we propose to use normalizing flows on the sphere (see Appendix
12.4.4
). We use two different normalizing flows, exponential map normalizing flows
(
Rezende et al. 2020
)
and Real NVP
(
Dinh et al. 2017
)
+ stereographic projection
(
Gemici et al. 2016
)
which we call “Stereo” in Table
6.1
.

To fit
T_{\#}\mu=p_{Z}
, we use either SSW, SW on the sphere, or SW on
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
for the stereographic projection based NF. For the exponential map normalizing flow, we compose 48 blocks, each one with 100 components. These transformations have 24000 parameters. For Real NVP, we compose 10 blocks of Real NVPs, with shifting and scaling as multilayer perceptrons, composed of 10 layers, 25 hidden units and with Leaky ReLU of parameters 0.2 for the activation function. The number of parameters of these networks is 27520.

For the training process, we perform 20000 epochs with full batch size. We use Adam as an optimizer with a learning rate of
10^{-1}
. For the stereographic NF, we use a learning rate of
10^{-3}
.

We report in Table
12.2
details of the datasets.

Table 12.2:
Details of Earth datasets.

Earthquake

Flood

Train set size

4284

8966

Data size

Sliced-Wasserstein Autoencoder

We recall that in the WAE framework, we want to minimize

\mathcal{L}(f,g)=\int c\big(x,g(f(x))\big)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\lambda D(f_{\#}\mu,p_{Z}),

(12.181)

where
f
is an encoder,
g
a decoder,
p_{Z}
a prior distribution,
c
some cost function and
D
is a divergence in the latent space. Several
D
were proposed. For example,
Tolstikhin et al. 2018
proposed to use the MMD,
Kolouri et al. 2019b
used the SW distance,
Patrini et al. 2020
used the Sinkhorn divergence,
Kolouri et al. 2019a
used the generalized SW distance. Here, we use
D=SSW_{2}^{2}
.

Architecture and procedure.

We first detail the hyperparameters and architectures of neural networks for MNIST and Fashion MNIST. For the encoder
f
and the decoder
g
, we use the same architecture as
Kolouri et al. 2019b
.

For both the encoder and the decoder architecture, we use fully convolutional architectures with 3x3 convolutional filters. More precisely, the architecture of the encoder is

\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{28\times 28}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv2d}_{16}\to\mathrm{LeakyReLU}_{0.2}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv2d}_{16}\to\mathrm{LeakyReLU}_{0.2}\to\mathrm{AvgPool}_{2}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv2d}_{32}\to\mathrm{LeakyReLU}_{0.2}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv2d}_{32}\to\mathrm{LeakyReLU}_{0.2}\to\mathrm{AvgPool}_{2}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv2d}_{64}\to\mathrm{LeakyReLU}_{0.2}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv2d}_{64}\to\mathrm{LeakyReLU}_{0.2}\to\mathrm{AvgPool}_{2}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Flatten}\to\mathrm{FC}_{128}\to\mathrm{ReLU}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{FC}_{d_{Z}}\to\ell^{2}\ \text{normalization}

where
d_{Z}
is the dimension of the latent space (either
11
for
S^{10}
or
3
for
S^{2}
).

The architecture of the decoder is

\displaystyle z\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{Z}}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{FC}_{128}\to\mathrm{FC}_{1024}\to\mathrm{ReLU}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Reshape(64x4x4)}\to\mathrm{Upsample}_{2}\to\mathrm{Conv}_{64}\to\mathrm{LeakyReLU}_{0.2}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv}_{64}\to\mathrm{LeakyReLU}_{0.2}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Upsample}_{2}\to\mathrm{Conv}_{64}\to\mathrm{LeakyReLU}_{0.2}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv}_{32}\to\mathrm{LeakyReLU}_{0.2}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Upsample}_{2}\to\mathrm{Conv}_{32}\to\mathrm{LeakyReLU}_{0.2}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv}_{1}\to\mathrm{Sigmoid}

To compare the different autoencoders, we used as the reconstruction loss the binary cross entropy,
\lambda=10
, Adam
(
Kingma and Ba 2015
)
as optimizer with a learning rate of
10^{-3}
and Pytorch’s default momentum parameters for 800 epochs with batch of size
n=500
. Moreover, when using SW type of distance, we approximated it with
L=1000
projections.

For the experiment on CIFAR10, we use the same architecture as
Tolstikhin et al. 2018
. More precisely, the architecture of the encoder is

\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 32\times 32}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv2d}_{128}\to\mathrm{BatchNorm}\to\mathrm{ReLU}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv2d}_{256}\to\mathrm{BatchNorm}\to\mathrm{ReLU}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv2d}_{512}\to\mathrm{BatchNorm}\to\mathrm{ReLU}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv2d}_{1024}\to\mathrm{BatchNorm}\to\mathrm{ReLU}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{FC}_{d_{z}}\to\ell^{2}\ \text{normalization}

where
d_{z}=65
.

The architecture of the decoder is

\displaystyle z\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{FC}_{4096}\to\mathrm{Reshape}(1024\times 2\times 2)

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv2dT}_{512}\to\mathrm{BatchNorm}\to\mathrm{ReLU}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv2dT}_{256}\to\mathrm{BatchNorm}\to\mathrm{ReLU}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv2dT}_{128}\to\mathrm{BatchNorm}\to\mathrm{ReLU}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Conv2dT}_{3}\to\mathrm{Sigmoid}

We use here a batch size of
n=128
,
\lambda=0.1
, the binary cross entropy as reconstruction loss and Adam as optimizer with a learning rate of
10^{-3}
.

We report in Table
6.2
the FID obtained using 10000 samples and we report the mean over 5 trainings.

For SSW, we used the formulation using the uniform distribution (
6.5
). To compute SW, we used the POT library
(
Flamary et al. 2021
)
. To compute the Sinkhorn divergence, we used the GeomLoss package
(
Feydy et al. 2019
)
.

12.5
Appendix of
Chapter

7

12.5.1
Proofs

First, we recall some propositions about the continuity and convexity of the Sliced-Wasserstein distance as well as on the existence of the minimizer at each step of the SW-JKO scheme. These results were derived in
(
Candau-Tilh 2020
)
. In the following, we restrain ourselves to measures supported on a compact domain
K
.

Proposition 12.2

(Proposition 3.4 in
(
Candau-Tilh 2020
)
)
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(K)
. Then,
\mu\mapsto\mathrm{SW}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)
is continuous
w.r.t.
the weak convergence.

Proposition 12.3

(Proposition 3.5 in
(
Candau-Tilh 2020
)
)
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(K)
, then
\mu\mapsto\mathrm{SW}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)
is convex and strictly convex whenever
\nu
is absolutely continuous
w.r.t.
the Lebesgue measure.

Proposition 12.4

(Proposition 3.7 in
(
Candau-Tilh 2020
)
)
.

Let
\tau>0
and
\mu_{k}^{\tau}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(K)
. Then, there exists a unique solution
\mu_{k+1}^{\tau}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(K)
to the minimization problem

\min_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(K)}\ \frac{\mathrm{SW}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\mu_{k}^{\tau})}{2\tau}+\int V\mathrm{d}\mu+\mathcal{H}(\mu).

(12.182)

The solution is even absolutely continuous.

Proof of
Proposition

7.1

Proof of
Proposition

7.1
.

Let
\tau>0
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mu_{k}^{\tau}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(K)
. Let’s note
J(\mu)=\frac{\mathrm{SW}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\mu_{k}^{\tau})}{2\tau}+\mathcal{F}(\mu)
.

According to
Proposition

12.2
,
\mu\mapsto\mathrm{SW}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\mu_{k}^{\tau})
is continuous with respect to the weak convergence. Indeed, let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(K)
and let
(\mu_{n})_{n}
converging weakly to
\mu
,
i.e.

\mu_{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\mathcal{L}}\mu
. Then, by the reverse triangular inequality, we have

|\mathrm{SW}_{2}(\mu_{n},\mu_{k}^{\tau})-\mathrm{SW}_{2}(\mu,\mu_{k}^{\tau})|\leq\mathrm{SW}_{2}(\mu_{n},\mu)\leq W_{2}(\mu_{n},\mu).

(12.183)

Since the Wasserstein distance metrizes the weak convergence
(
Villani 2009
)
, we have that
W_{2}(\mu_{n},\mu)\to 0
. And therefore,
\mu\mapsto\mathrm{SW}_{2}(\mu,\mu_{k}^{\tau})
is continuous
w.r.t.
the weak convergence.

By hypothesis,
\mathcal{F}
is lower semi continuous, hence
\mu\mapsto J(\mu)
is lower semi continuous. Moreover,
\mathcal{P}_{2}(K)
is compact for the weak convergence, thus we can apply the Weierstrass theorem (Box 1.1 in
(
Santambrogio 2015
)
) and there exists a minimizer
\mu_{k+1}^{\tau}
of
J
.

By
Proposition

12.3
,
\mu\mapsto\mathrm{SW}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)
is convex and strictly convex whenever
\nu
is absolutely continuous
w.r.t.
the Lebesgue measure. Hence, for the uniqueness, if
\mathcal{F}
is strictly convex then
\mu\mapsto J(\mu)
is also strictly convex and the minimizer is unique. And if
\rho_{k}^{\tau}
is absolutely continuous, then according to
Proposition

12.3
,
\mu\mapsto\mathrm{SW}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\mu_{k}^{\tau})
is strictly convex, and hence
\mu\mapsto J(\mu)
is also strictly convex since
\mathcal{F}
was taken convex by hypothesis.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.2

Proof of
Proposition

7.2
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then since
\mu_{k+1}^{\tau}
is the minimizer of (
7.31
),

\mathcal{F}(\mu_{k+1}^{\tau})+\frac{\mathrm{SW}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{k+1}^{\tau},\mu_{k}^{\tau})}{2\tau}\leq\mathcal{F}(\mu_{k}^{\tau})+\frac{\mathrm{SW}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{k}^{\tau},\mu_{k}^{\tau})}{2\tau}=\mathcal{F}(\mu_{k}^{\tau}).

(12.184)

Hence, as
\mathrm{SW}_{2}^{2}(\mu_{k+1}^{\tau},\mu_{k}^{\tau})\geq 0
,

\mathcal{F}(\mu_{k+1}^{\tau})\leq\mathcal{F}(\mu_{k}^{\tau}).

(12.185)

∎

12.5.2
Relations between Sliced-Wasserstein and Wasserstein

Link for 1D supported measures.

Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
supported on a line. For simplicity, we suppose that the measures are supported on an axis,
i.e.

\mu(x)=\mu_{1}(x_{1})\prod_{i=2}^{d}\delta_{0}(x_{i})
and
\nu(x)=\nu_{1}(x_{1})\prod_{i=2}^{d}\delta_{0}(x_{i})
.

In this case, we have that

W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)=W_{2}^{2}(P^{e_{1}}_{\#}\mu,P^{e_{1}}_{\#}\nu)=\int_{0}^{1}|F_{P^{e_{1}}_{\#}\mu}^{-1}(x)-F_{P^{e_{1}}_{\#}\nu}^{-1}(x)|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}x.

(12.186)

On the other hand, let
\theta\in S^{d-1}
, then we have

\displaystyle\forall y\in\mathbb{R},\ F_{P^{\theta}_{\#}\mu}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{1}_{]-\infty,y]}(x)\ P^{\theta}_{\#}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)

(12.187)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{1}_{]-\infty,y]}(\langle\theta,x\rangle)\ \mu(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{1}_{]-\infty,y]}(x_{1}\theta_{1})\ \mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{1}_{]-\infty,\frac{y}{\theta_{1}}]}(x_{1})\ \mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x_{1})

\displaystyle=F_{P^{e_{1}}_{\#}\mu}\left(\frac{y}{\theta_{1}}\right).

Therefore,
F_{P^{\theta}_{\#}\mu}^{-1}(z)=\theta_{1}F_{P^{e_{1}}_{\#}\mu}^{-1}(z)
and

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(P^{\theta}_{\#}\mu,P^{\theta}_{\#}\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}|\theta_{1}F_{P^{e_{1}}_{\#}\mu}^{-1}(z)-\theta_{1}F_{P^{e_{1}}_{\#}\nu}^{-1}(z)|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}z

(12.188)

\displaystyle=\theta_{1}^{2}\int_{0}^{1}|F_{P^{e_{1}}_{\#}\mu}^{-1}(z)-F_{P^{e_{1}}_{\#}\nu}^{-1}(z)|^{2}\ \mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\theta_{1}^{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu).

Finally, using that
\int_{S^{d-1}}\theta\theta^{T}\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(\theta)=\frac{1}{d}I_{d}
, we can conclude that

SW_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)=\int_{S^{d-1}}\theta_{1}^{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(\theta)=\frac{W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)}{d}.

(12.189)

Closed-form between Gaussians.

It is well known that there is a closed-form for the Wasserstein distance between Gaussians
(
Givens and Shortt 1984
)
. If we take
\alpha=\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
and
\beta=\mathcal{N}(m,\Lambda)
with
m,\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\Sigma,\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
two symmetric positive definite matrices, then

W_{2}^{2}(\alpha,\beta)=\|m-\mu\|_{2}^{2}+\mathrm{Tr}\big(\Sigma+\Lambda-2(\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big).

(12.190)

Let
\alpha=\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}I_{d})
and
\beta=\mathcal{N}(m,s^{2}I_{d})
two isotropic Gaussians. Here, we have

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\|\mu-m\|^{2}_{2}+\mathrm{Tr}(\sigma^{2}I_{d}+s^{2}I_{d}-2(\sigma s^{2}\sigma I_{d})^{\frac{1}{2}})

(12.191)

\displaystyle=\|\mu-m\|^{2}_{2}+(\sigma-s)^{2}\ \mathrm{Tr}(I_{d})

\displaystyle=\|\mu-m\|^{2}_{2}+d(\sigma-s)^{2}.

On the other hand,
Nadjahi et al. 2021
showed (Equation 73) that

SW_{2}^{2}(\alpha,\beta)=\frac{\|\mu-m\|_{2}^{2}}{d}+(\sigma-s)^{2}=\frac{W_{2}^{2}(\alpha,\beta)}{d}.

(12.192)

In that case, the dilation of factor
d
between WGF and SWGF clearly appears.

12.5.3
Algorithms to solve the SW-JKO scheme

We provide here the algorithms used to solve the SW-JKO scheme (
7.31
) for the discrete grid and for the particles (
Section

7.3.3
).

Discrete grid.

Algorithm 12.1

SW-JKO with Discrete Grid

Input:

\mu_{0}
the initial distribution with density
\rho_{0}
,
K
the number of SW-JKO steps,
\tau
the step size,
\mathcal{F}
the functional,
N_{e}
the number of epochs to solve each SW-JKO step,
(x_{j})_{j=1}^{N}
the grid

Let
\rho^{(0)}=\left(\frac{\rho_{0}(x_{j})}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\rho_{0}(x_{\ell})}\right)_{j=1}^{N}

for

k=1

to

K

do

Initialize the weights
\rho^{(k+1)}
(with for example a copy of
\rho^{(k)}
)

// Denote
\mu_{k+1}^{\tau}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\rho_{j}^{(k+1)}\delta_{x_{j}}
and
\mu_{k}^{\tau}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\rho_{j}^{(k)}\delta_{x_{j}}

for

i=1

to

N_{e}

do

Compute
J(\mu_{k+1}^{\tau})=\frac{1}{2\tau}SW_{2}^{2}(\mu_{k}^{\tau},\mu_{k+1}^{\tau})+\mathcal{F}(\mu_{k+1}^{\tau})

Backpropagate through
J
with respect to
\rho^{(k+1)}

Perform a gradient step

Project on the simplex
\rho^{(k+1)}
using the algorithm of
Condat 2016

end

for

end

for

We recall that in that case, we model the distributions as
\mu_{k}^{\tau}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho_{i}^{(k)}\delta_{x_{i}}
where we use
N
samples located at
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
and
(\rho_{i}^{(k)})_{i=1}^{N}
belongs to the simplex
\Sigma_{n}
. Hence, the SW-JKO scheme at step
k+1
rewrites

\min_{(\rho_{i})_{i}\in\Sigma_{N}}\ \frac{SW_{2}^{2}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho_{i}\delta_{x_{i}},\mu_{k}^{\tau})}{2\tau}+\mathcal{F}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}).

(12.193)

We report in Algorithm
12.1
the whole procedure.

Particle scheme.

In this case, we model the distributions as empirical distributions and we try to optimize the positions of the particles. Hence, we have
\mu_{k}^{\tau}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}^{(k)}}
and the problem (
7.31
) becomes

\min_{(x_{i})_{i}}\ \frac{SW_{2}^{2}(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}},\mu_{k}^{\tau})}{2\tau}+\mathcal{F}(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}).

(12.194)

In this case, we provide the procedure in Algorithm
12.2
.

Algorithm 12.2

SW-JKO with Particles

Input:

\mu_{0}
the initial distribution,
K
the number of SW-JKO steps,
\tau
the step size,
\mathcal{F}
the functional,
N_{e}
the number of epochs to solve each SW-JKO step,
N
the batch size

Sample
(x^{(0)}_{j})_{j=1}^{N}\sim\mu_{0}
i.i.d

for

k=1

to

K

do

Initialize
N
particles
(x^{(k+1)}_{j})_{j=1}^{N}
(with for example a copy of
(x^{(k)}_{j})_{j=1}^{N}
)

// Denote
\mu_{k+1}^{\tau}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{j}^{(k+1)}}
and
\mu_{k}^{\tau}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{j}^{(k)}}

for

i=1

to

N_{e}

do

Compute
J(\mu_{k+1}^{\tau})=\frac{1}{2\tau}SW_{2}^{2}(\mu_{k}^{\tau},\mu_{k+1}^{\tau})+\mathcal{F}(\mu_{k+1}^{\tau})

Backpropagate through
J
with respect to
(x_{j}^{(k+1)})_{j=1}^{N}

Perform a gradient step

end

for

end

for

12.5.4
Experimental Details

Convergence to stationary distribution

Here, we want to demonstrate that, through the SW-JKO scheme, we are able to find good minima of functionals using simple generative models.

Figure 12.7:
Symmetric KL divergence between the learned distribution at time
t=8
and the true stationary solution on Gaussians

In this experiment, we generate 15 Gaussians for
d
between 2 and 12, and we quantify how well the SW-JKO scheme, using a Real NVP to approximate distributions, is able to learn the target Gaussians. We start from
\mu_{0}=\mathcal{N}(0,I)
and use a step size of
\tau=0.1
for 80 iterations in order to match the stationary distribution. In this case, the functional is

\mathcal{F}(\mu)=\int V(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)+\mathcal{H}(\mu)

(12.195)

with
V(x)=-\frac{1}{2}(x-b)^{T}A(x-b)
, and the stationary distribution is
\rho^{*}(x)\propto e^{-V(x)}
, hence
\rho^{*}=\mathcal{N}(b,A^{-1})
. This functional is approximated using (
7.40
). In
Figure

7.5
, we showed the results for
d\in\{2,\dots,12\}
and the unstability of JKO-ICNN. We add the results for
d\in\{20,30,40,50,75,100\}
in
Figure

12.7
.

Symmetric Kullback-Leibler divergence.

To quantify the closeness between the learned distributions and the targets, we compute the symmetric Kullback-Leibler divergence between the ground truth of WGF
\mu^{*}
and the distribution
\hat{\mu}
approximated by the different schemes. The symmetric Kullback-Leibler divergence is obtained as

\mathrm{SymKL}(\mu^{*},\hat{\mu})=\mathrm{KL}(\mu^{*}||\hat{\mu})+\mathrm{KL}(\hat{\mu}||\mu^{*}).

(12.196)

To approximate it, we generate
10^{4}
samples of each distribution and evaluate the density at those samples.

Normalizing flows.

If we note
g_{\theta}
a normalizing flows,
p_{Z}
the distribution in the latent space and
\rho=(g_{\theta})_{\#}p_{Z}
, then we can evaluate the log density of
\rho
by using the change of variable formula. Let
x=g_{\theta}(z)
, then

\log(\rho(x))=\log(p_{Z}(z))-\log|\det J_{g_{\theta}}(z)|.

(12.197)

We choose RealNVPs
(
Dinh et al. 2017
)
for the simplicity of the transformations and the fact that we can compute efficiently the determinant of the Jacobian (since we have a closed-form). A RealNVP flow is a composition of transformations
T
of the form

\forall z\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ x=T(z)=\big(z^{1},\exp(s(z^{1}))\odot z^{2}+t(z^{1})\big)

(12.198)

where we write
z=(z^{1},z^{2})
and with
s
and
t
some neural networks. To modify all the components, we use also swap transformations (
i.e.

(z^{1},z^{2})\mapsto(z^{2},z^{1})
). This transformation is invertible with
\log\det J_{T}(z)=\sum_{i}s(z^{1}_{i})
.

In our experiments, we use RealNVPs with 5 affine coupling layers, using fully connected neural networks for the scaling and shifting networks with 100 hidden units and 5 layers.

Optimization hyperparameters.

We use 200 epochs of each inner optimization and an Adam optimizer
(
Kingma and Ba 2015
)
with a learning rate of
5\cdot 10^{-3}
for the first iteration and
10^{-3}
for the rest. We also use a batch size of 1000 samples. To approximate
\mathrm{SW}
, we always use
L=1000
projections.

Euler-Maruyama.

For Euler-Maruyama, as in
(
Mokrov et al. 2021
)
, we use kernel density estimation in order to approximate the density. We use the
Scipy
implementation
(
Virtanen et al. 2020
)
“gaussian_kde” with the Scott’s rule to choose the bandwidth. We run the different chains with a step size of
10^{-3}
.

Bayesian logistic regression

For the Bayesian logistic regression, we have access to covariates
s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with their associated labels
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\in\{-1,1\}
. Following
(
Liu and Wang 2016
,
Mokrov et al. 2021
)
, we put as prior on the regression weights
w
,
p_{0}(w|\alpha)=\mathcal{N}(w;0,\frac{1}{\alpha})
with
p_{0}(\alpha)=\Gamma(\alpha;1,0.01)
. Therefore, we aim at learning the posterior
p(w,\alpha|y)
:

p(w,\alpha|y)\propto p(y|w,\alpha)p_{0}(w|\alpha)p_{0}(\alpha)=p_{0}(\alpha)p_{0}(w|\alpha)\prod_{i=1}^{n}p(y_{i}|w,\alpha)

(12.199)

where
p(y_{i}|w,\alpha)=\sigma(w^{T}s_{i})^{\frac{1+y_{i}}{2}}(1-\sigma(w^{T}s_{i}))^{\frac{1-y}{2}}
with
\sigma
the sigmoid. To evaluate
\mathcal{V}(\mu)=\int V(x)\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)
, we resample data uniformly.

In our context, let
V(x)=-\log\big(p_{0}(\alpha)p_{0}(w|\alpha)p(y|w,\alpha)\big)
, then using
\mathcal{F}(\mu)=\int V\mathrm{d}\mu+\mathcal{H}(\mu)
as functional, we know that the limit of the stationary solution of Fokker-Planck is proportional to
e^{-V}=p(w,\alpha|y)
.

Following
Liu and Wang 2016
,
Mokrov et al. 2021
, we use the 8 datasets of
Mika et al. 1999
and the covertype dataset (
https://www.csie.ntu.edu.tw/˜cjlin/libsvmtools/datasets/binary.html
).

We report in Table
12.3
the characteristics of the different datasets. The datasets are loaded using the code of
Mokrov et al. 2021
(
https://github.com/PetrMokrov/Large-Scale-Wasserstein-Gradient-Flows
). We split the dataset between train set and test set with a 4:1 ratio.

, we observed bad results, as the SW-JKO scheme should be a good approximation of the SWGF only for small enough
\tau
.

.

Influence of the number of projections

It is well known that the approximation of Sliced-Wasserstein is subject to the curse of dimensionality through the Monte-Carlo approximation
(
Nadjahi et al. 2020b
)
. We provide here some experiments to quantify this influence. However, first note that the goal is not to minimize the Sliced-Wasserstein distance, but rather the functional, SW playing mostly a regularizer role. Experiments on the influence of the number of experiments to approximate the SW have already been conducted (see
e.g.
Figure 2 in
(
Nadjahi et al. 2020b
)
or Figure 1 in
(
Deshpande et al. 2019
)
).

Here, we take the same setting of Section
7.5.1
,
i.e.
we generate 15 random Gaussians, and then vary the number of projections and report the Symmetric Kullback-Leibler divergence on Figure
7.6
. We observe that the results seem to improve with the number of projections until they reach a certain plateau. The plateau seems to be attained for a bigger number of dimensions in high dimensions.

Document Classification

Datasets

We sum up the statistics of the different datasets in
Table

12.5
.

BBCSport.

The BBCSport dataset contains articles between 2004 and 2005, and is composed of 5 classes. We average over the 5 same train/test split of
Kusner et al. 2015
. The dataset can be found in
https://github.com/mkusner/wmd/tree/master
.

Movie Reviews.

The movie reviews dataset is composed of 1000 positive and 1000 negative reviews. We take five different random 75/25 train/test split. The data can be found in
http://www.cs.cornell.edu/people/pabo/movie-review-data/
.

Goodreads.

This dataset, proposed in
(
Maharjan et al. 2017
)
, and which can be found at
https://ritual.uh.edu/multi_task_book_success_2017/
, is composed of 1003 books from 8 genres. A first possible classification task is to predict the genre. A second task is to predict the likability, which is a binary task where a book is said to have success if it has an average rating
\geq 3.5
on the website Goodreads (
https://www.goodreads.com
). The five train/test split are randomly drawn with 75/25 proportions.

Technical Details

All documents are embedded with the Word2Vec model
(
Mikolov et al. 2013
)
in dimension
d=300
. The embedding can be found in
https://drive.google.com/file/d/0B7XkCwpI5KDYNlNUTTlSS21pQmM/view?resourcekey=0-wjGZdNAUop6WykTtMip30g
.

In this experiment, we report the results averaged over 5 random train/test split. For discrepancies which are approximated using random projections, we additionally average the results over 3 different computations, and we report this standard deviation in
Figure

8.3
. Furthermore, we always use 500 projections to approximate the sliced discrepancies. For Frank-Wolfe based methods, we use 10 iterations, which we found to be enough to have a good accuracy. We added an ablation of these two hyperparameters in Figure
8.5
. We report the results obtained with the best
\rho
for
USW
and
SUOT
computed among a grid
\rho\in\{10^{-4},5\cdot 10^{-4},10^{-3},5\cdot 10^{-3},10^{-2},10^{-1},1\}
. For
USW
, the best
\rho
is consistently
5\cdot 10^{-3}
for the Movies and Goodreads datasets, and
5\cdot 10^{-4}
for the BBCSport dataset. For
SUOT
, the best
\rho
obtained was
0.01
for the BBCSport dataset,
1.0
for the movies dataset and
0.5
for the goodreads dataset. For UOT, we used
\rho=1.0
on the BBCSport dataset. For the movies dataset, the best
\rho
obtained on a subset was 50, but it took an unreasonable amount of time to run on the full dataset as the runtime increases with
\rho
(see
(
Chapel et al. 2021
, Figure 3)
). On the goodreads dataset, it took too much memory on the GPU. For Sinkhorn UOT, we used
\epsilon=0.001
and
\rho=0.1
on the BBCSport and Goodreads datasets, and
\epsilon=0.01
on the Movies dataset. For each method, the number of neighbors used for the k-NN method is obtained via cross-validation.

12.7
Appendix of
Chapter

9

12.7.1
Proofs of
Section

9.2

Proof of
Proposition

9.1

Proof of
Proposition

9.1
.

In the setting
c(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}\|x-y\|_{2}^{2}
and
\mu_{0}
absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, we can apply Brenier’s theorem (
Theorem

2.1
) and hence there is a unique OT map
T
between
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
, and
T
is the gradient of a convex function,
i.e.

T=\nabla u
with
u
convex.

First, let us suppose that the OT map
T
between
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
is the gradient of a 1-convex function
u
. Let
\mu:t\mapsto\big((1-t)\mathrm{Id}+tT)_{\#}\mu_{0}=\big((1-t)\mathrm{Id}+t\nabla u\big)_{\#}\mu_{0}
. Then, on one hand, we have

W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s},\mu_{t})\leq(t-s)^{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).

(12.200)

Indeed, let
\gamma^{*}\in\Pi(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
be an optimal coupling. Then, necessarily, denoting
\pi^{s}(x,y)=(1-s)x+sy
, we have for any
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
,
(\pi^{s},\pi^{t})_{\#}\gamma^{*}\in\Pi(\mu_{s},\mu_{t})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s},\mu_{t})

\displaystyle\leq\int\|x-y\|_{2}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}(\pi^{s},\pi^{t})_{\#}\gamma^{*}(x,y)

(12.201)

\displaystyle=\int\|(1-s)x+sy-(1-t)x-ty\|_{2}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma^{*}(x,y)

\displaystyle=(s-t)^{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}).

Then, let
\alpha\geq 1
and
0\leq s<t\leq\alpha
. By the triangular inequality and the previous inequality, we have

\displaystyle W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\leq W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{s})+W_{2}(\mu_{s},\mu_{t})+W_{2}(\mu_{t},\mu_{\alpha})

(12.202)

\displaystyle=(s+\alpha-t)W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+W_{2}(\mu_{s},\mu_{t}).

If
x\mapsto(1-\alpha)\frac{\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2}+\alpha u(x)
is convex (
i.e.

u
is
\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}
-convex), then its gradient which is equal to
x\mapsto(1-\alpha)x+\alpha\nabla u(x)
is the Monge map between
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{\alpha}
as
\mu_{\alpha}=\big((1-\alpha)\mathrm{Id}+\alpha\nabla u\big)_{\#}\mu_{0}
, and thus
W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{\alpha})=\alpha^{2}W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
. Hence, we obtain

W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{\alpha})=\alpha W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\leq(s+\alpha-t)W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+W_{2}(\mu_{s},\mu_{t})\iff(t-s)W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\leq W_{2}(\mu_{s},\mu_{t}).

(12.203)

It allows to conclude that
W_{2}(\mu_{s},\mu_{t})=|t-s|W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
for all
s,t\in[0,\alpha]
.
To extend it on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we need it to be true for all
\alpha\geq 1
, which is true as
u
is 1-convex. Thus, we can conclude that
t\mapsto\mu_{t}
is a geodesic ray.

For the opposite direction, suppose that
\mu_{t}=\big((1-t)\mathrm{Id}+tT\big)_{\#}\mu_{0}
is a geodesic ray. Then, for all
s\geq 0
,

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s},\mu_{0})

\displaystyle=s^{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

(12.204)

\displaystyle=\int\|s(x-\nabla u(x))\|_{2}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\int\|x-(1-s)x-s\nabla u(x)\|_{2}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\int\|x-T_{s}(x)\|_{2}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\mu_{0}(x),

where
(T_{s})_{\#}\mu_{0}=\mu_{s}
with
T_{s}:x\mapsto(1-s)x+\nabla u(x)
. By Brenier’s theorem, since the OT map is unique and necessarily the gradient of a convex functions,
T_{s}=\nabla u_{s}
with
u_{s}:x\mapsto(1-s)\frac{\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2}+su(x)=\frac{\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2}+s\left(u(x)-\frac{\|x\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\right)
convex.
Thus, for all
s\geq 0
,

I+s(\nabla^{2}u-I)\succeq 0\iff\nabla^{2}u-I\succeq-\frac{1}{s}I.

(12.205)

It is true for all
s\geq 0
, hence taking the limit
s\to\infty
, we obtain well
\nabla^{2}u-I\succeq 0
,
i.e.

u
is 1-convex.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

9.2

Proof of
Proposition

9.2
.

By
(
Ambrosio et al. 2008
, Equation 7.2.8)
, the quantile of
\mu_{t}
is
F_{t}^{-1}=(1-t)F_{0}^{-1}+tF_{1}^{-1}
. Then, we know that
F_{t}^{-1}
is a quantile function if and only if it is non-decreasing and continuous. As a linear combination of continuous function, it is always continuous. We only need to find conditions for which it is non-decreasing for all
t\geq 0
.
Let
0<m<m^{\prime}<1
, then

\displaystyle F_{t}^{-1}(m)-F_{t}^{-1}(m^{\prime})=F_{0}^{-1}(m)-F_{0}^{-1}(m^{\prime})+t\big(F_{1}^{-1}(m)-F_{0}^{-1}(m)-F_{1}^{-1}(m^{\prime})+F_{0}^{-1}(m^{\prime})\big),

(12.206)

and hence,

\displaystyle\forall t\geq 0,m^{\prime}>m,\ F_{t}^{-1}(m)-F_{t}^{-1}(m^{\prime})\leq 0

\displaystyle\iff\forall m^{\prime}>m,\ F_{1}^{-1}(m)-F_{0}^{-1}(m)\leq F_{1}^{-1}(m^{\prime})-F_{0}^{-1}(m^{\prime})

(12.207)

\displaystyle\iff F_{1}^{-1}-F_{0}^{-1}\text{ non-decreasing}.

∎

12.7.2
Proof of
Section

9.3

Proof of
Proposition

9.3

Proof of
Proposition

9.3
.

(\mu_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a unit-speed ray. Therefore,
W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=1
.

Then, we have, for any
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle W_{2}(\nu,\mu_{t})-t

(12.208)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\nu}^{-1}(u)-F_{\mu_{t}}^{-1}(u)\big)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}u\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-t

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\nu}^{-1}(u)-(1-t)F_{\mu_{0}}^{-1}(u)-tF_{\mu_{1}}^{-1}(u)\big)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}u\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-t

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\nu}^{-1}(u)-F_{\mu_{0}}^{-1}(u)+t(F_{\mu_{0}}^{-1}(u)-F_{\mu_{1}}^{-1}(u))\big)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}u\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-t

\displaystyle=\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\nu}^{-1}(u)-F_{\mu_{0}}^{-1}(u)\big)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}u+2t\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\nu}^{-1}(u)-F_{\mu_{0}}^{-1}(u)\big)\big(F_{\mu_{0}}^{-1}(u)-F_{\mu_{1}}^{-1}(u)\big)\ \mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle+t^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\mu_{0}}^{-1}(u)-F_{\mu_{1}}^{-1}(u)\big)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}u\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}-t

\displaystyle=t\left(\frac{1}{t^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\nu}^{-1}(u)-F_{\mu_{0}}^{-1}(u)\big)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}u+\frac{2}{t}\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\nu}^{-1}(u)-F_{\mu_{0}}^{-1}(u)\big)\big(F_{\mu_{0}}^{-1}(u)-F_{\mu_{1}}^{-1}(u)\big)\ \mathrm{d}u+W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-t

\displaystyle\underset{t\to\infty}{=}t\left(1+\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\nu}^{-1}(u)-F_{\mu_{0}}^{-1}(u)\big)\big(F_{\mu_{0}}^{-1}(u)-F_{\mu_{1}}^{-1}(u)\big)\ \mathrm{d}u+o\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\right)-t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\big(F_{\nu}^{-1}(u)-F_{\mu_{0}}^{-1}(u)\big)\big(F_{\mu_{0}}^{-1}(u)-F_{\mu_{1}}^{-1}(u)\big)\ \mathrm{d}u.

∎

Proofs of
Proposition

9.4

Proof of
Proposition

9.4
.

We will use here that for any geodesic ray
\gamma
,
\lim_{t\to\infty}\ \frac{d(x,\gamma(t))+t}{2t}=1
(cf
(
Bridson and Haefliger 2013
, II. 8.24)
. Then we know that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\ \frac{d(x,\gamma(t))^{2}-t^{2}}{2t}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\ \big(d(x,\gamma(t))-t\big),

(12.209)

since

\frac{d(x,\gamma(t))^{2}-t^{2}}{2t}=\frac{(d(x,\gamma(t))-t)(d(x,\gamma(t))+t)}{2t}=\big(d(x,\gamma(t))-t\big)\frac{d(x,\gamma(t))+t}{2t}\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{}B_{\gamma}(x).

(12.210)

In our case, we have for any
t\geq 0
,
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{N}(m_{t},\Sigma_{t})
where

\begin{cases}m_{t}=(1-t)m_{0}+tm_{1}\\
\Sigma_{t}=\big((1-t)I_{d}+tA\big)\Sigma_{0}\big((1-t)I_{d}+tA\big),\end{cases}

(12.211)

with
A=\Sigma_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Sigma_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Then, we have, using in particular that
A\Sigma_{0}A=\Sigma_{1}
, for any
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\|m_{t}-m\|_{2}^{2}}{2t}=\frac{t}{2}\|m_{1}-m_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+\langle m_{1}-m_{0},m_{0}-m\rangle+O\left(\frac{1}{t}\right),

(12.212)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Tr}(\Sigma_{t})}{2t}=\frac{t}{2}\mathrm{Tr}\big(\Sigma_{0}-2\Sigma_{0}A+\Sigma_{1}\big)+\mathrm{Tr}\big(\Sigma_{0}A-\Sigma_{0}\big)+O\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)

(12.213)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Tr}\big((\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Sigma_{t}\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)}{2t}=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{0}-\Sigma_{0}A-A\Sigma_{0}+\Sigma_{1})\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}+O\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).

(12.214)

Then, by remembering that

W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})=\|m_{1}-m_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+\mathrm{Tr}(\Sigma_{0}+\Sigma_{1}-2(\Sigma_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\frac{1}{2}})=1,

(12.215)

and using that

\Sigma_{0}A=\Sigma_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Sigma_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}

(12.216)

and hence

\mathrm{Tr}(\Sigma_{0}A)=\mathrm{Tr}\big((\Sigma_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big),

(12.217)

we obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{W_{2}^{2}(\nu,\mu_{t})-t^{2}}{2t}

(12.218)

\displaystyle=\frac{\|m_{t}-m\|_{2}^{2}+\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Sigma_{t}+\Sigma-2(\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Sigma_{t}\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)-t^{2}}{2t}

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{2}\left(\|m_{1}-m_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+\mathrm{Tr}(\Sigma_{0}+\Sigma_{1}-2\Sigma_{0}A)\right)+\langle m_{1}-m_{0},m_{0}-m\rangle+\mathrm{Tr}\big(\Sigma_{0}A-\Sigma_{0}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{0}-\Sigma_{0}A-A\Sigma_{0}+\Sigma_{1})\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}+O\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)-\frac{t}{2}+O\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+\langle m_{1}-m_{0},m_{0}-m\rangle+\mathrm{Tr}\big(\Sigma_{0}A-\Sigma_{0}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{0}-\Sigma_{0}A-A\Sigma_{0}+\Sigma_{1})\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}+O\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)-\frac{t}{2}+O\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\langle m_{1}-m_{0},m_{0}-m\rangle+\mathrm{Tr}\big(\Sigma_{0}A-\Sigma_{0}\big)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{0}-\Sigma_{0}A-A\Sigma_{0}+\Sigma_{1})\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}+O\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{}\langle m_{1}-m_{0},m_{0}-m\rangle+\mathrm{Tr}\big(\Sigma_{0}(A-I_{d})\big)-\mathrm{Tr}\big((\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{0}-\Sigma_{0}A-A\Sigma_{0}+\Sigma_{1})\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big).

∎

12.7.3
Proofs of
Section

9.4

Proof of
Proposition

9.5

Proof of
Proposition

9.5
.

First, let us find the first component. We want to solve:

\displaystyle\max_{(m,\sigma)}\ \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big((m-m_{0})(m_{i}-m_{0})+(\sigma-\sigma_{0})(\sigma_{i}-\sigma_{0})\big)^{2}-\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big((m-m_{0})(m_{i}-m_{0})+(\sigma-\sigma_{0})(\sigma_{i}-\sigma_{0})\big)\right)^{2}

(12.219)

\displaystyle\text{subject to }\begin{cases}(m-m_{0})^{2}+(\sigma-\sigma_{0})^{2}=1\\
\sigma-\sigma_{0}\geq 0.\end{cases}

Let’s note for all
i
,
x_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}m_{i}-m_{0}\\
\sigma_{i}-\sigma_{0}\end{pmatrix}
and
x=\begin{pmatrix}m-m_{0}\\
\sigma-\sigma_{0}\end{pmatrix}
. Then, the objective can be rewritten as

\max_{x}\ \frac{1}{n}x^{T}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{T}\right)x-x^{T}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\right)^{T}x\quad\text{subject to}\quad\begin{cases}\|x\|_{2}^{2}=1\\
[x]_{2}\geq 0.\end{cases}

(12.220)

This is a convex objective on a compact space. Let’s encode the constraints by using a parametrization on the circle. Indeed, as
\|x\|_{2}^{2}=1
and
[x]_{2}\geq 0
, there exists
\theta\in[0,\pi]
such that
x=x_{\theta}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta\\
\sin\theta\end{pmatrix}
.
Now, let
M=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{T}-\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\right)^{T}
and rewrite the objective as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}x_{\theta}^{T}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{T}\right)x_{\theta}-x_{\theta}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\right)^{T}x_{\theta}

\displaystyle=x_{\theta}^{T}Mx_{\theta}

(12.221)

\displaystyle=\cos^{2}(\theta)M_{11}+\sin^{2}(\theta)M_{22}+2\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)M_{12}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\cos(2\theta)}{2}M_{11}+\frac{1-\cos(2\theta)}{2}M_{22}+\sin(2\theta)M_{12}

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{11}-M_{22}}{2}\cos(2\theta)+\frac{1}{2}(M_{11}+M_{22})+\sin(2\theta)M_{12}.

Thus the problem is equivalent with

\max_{\tilde{\theta}\in[0,2\pi[}\ \frac{M_{11}-M_{22}}{2}\cos(\tilde{\theta})+M_{12}\sin(\tilde{\theta})=f(\tilde{\theta}).

(12.222)

Noting
a=\frac{M_{11}-M_{22}}{2}
,
b=M_{12}
, and posing
\phi
such that
\cos\phi=\frac{a}{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}}
and
\sin\phi=\frac{b}{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}}
, we can rewrite
f
as

f(\tilde{\theta})=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}\big(\cos\phi\cos\tilde{\theta}+\sin\phi\sin\tilde{\theta}\big)=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}\cos(\tilde{\theta}-\phi).

(12.223)

Hence,
f
is maximal for
\tilde{\theta}=\phi=\arccos\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}}\right)=\arccos\left(\frac{M_{11}-M_{22}}{\sqrt{(M_{11}-M_{22})^{2}+4M_{12}^{2}}}\right)
.
Finally, we get the optimum as

\begin{cases}m^{(1)}_{1}=m_{0}+\cos\left(\frac{\tilde{\theta}}{2}\right)\\
\sigma^{(1)}_{1}=\sigma_{0}+\sin\left(\frac{\tilde{\theta}}{2}\right).\end{cases}

(12.224)

The second component is fully characterized by the orthogonal condition. Noting
\psi\in[0,\pi[
the angle such that
x_{\psi}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\phi\\
\sin\psi\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}m-m_{0}\\
\sigma-\sigma_{0}\end{pmatrix}
is the solution of

\max_{\psi\in[0,\pi]}\ x_{\psi}^{T}Mx_{\psi}\quad\text{subject to}\quad\langle x_{\theta},x_{\psi}\rangle=0.

(12.225)

Then,
\langle x_{\theta},x_{\psi}\rangle=0\iff\cos(\theta-\psi)=0\iff\psi=\theta\pm\frac{\pi}{2}
. Since
\psi\in[0,\pi[
, if
\theta\geq\frac{\pi}{2}
then
\psi=\theta-\frac{\pi}{2}
. If
\theta<\frac{\pi}{2}
, then
\psi=\theta+\frac{\pi}{2}
. To conclude, the second component is obtained with

\begin{cases}m_{1}^{(2)}=m_{0}+\cos\left(\frac{\tilde{\theta}-\mathrm{sign}(\tilde{\theta}-\pi)\pi}{2}\right)\\
\sigma_{1}^{(2)}=\sigma_{0}+\sin\left(\frac{\tilde{\theta}-\mathrm{sign}(\tilde{\theta}-\pi)\pi}{2}\right).\end{cases}

(12.226)

∎

Proof of
Proposition

9.6

Proof of
Proposition

9.6
.

Let
\mu_{0}=\mathcal{N}(m_{0},\sigma_{0}^{2})
and
\mu_{1}=\mathcal{N}(m_{1},\sigma_{1}^{2})
such that
(m_{1}-m_{0})^{2}+(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0})^{2}=1
and
\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{0}
. Extending the geodesic between
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
on
[1-\alpha,0]
for
\alpha\geq 1
is equivalent to extending the geodesic between
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{0}
on
[0,\alpha]
. Thus, we first find a condition to extend the geodesic between
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{0}
.

The Monge map
\tilde{T}
between
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{0}
is defined for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
as
\tilde{T}(x)=\frac{\sigma_{0}}{\sigma_{1}}(x-m_{1})+m_{0}=h^{\prime}(x)
with
h:x\mapsto\frac{\sigma_{0}}{\sigma_{1}}(x-m_{1})^{2}+m_{0}x
. Then, by
(
Natale et al. 2022
, Section 4)
, we know that we can extend the geodesic linking
\mu_{1}
to
\mu_{0}
on
[0,\alpha]
for
\alpha\geq 1
if and only if
h
is
\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}
-convex,
i.e.
if and only if

h^{\prime\prime}(x)-\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}\geq 0\iff\frac{\sigma_{0}}{\sigma_{1}}\geq\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}\iff\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{0}}\leq\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}.

(12.227)

Therefore, we deduce that we can extend the geodesic ray starting from
\mu_{0}
and passing through
\mu_{1}
at
t=1
on
[-(\alpha-1),+\infty[
if and only if
\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}\geq\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{0}}\geq 1
(the last inequality comes from the condition to have a geodesic ray
\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{0}
).

Since
(m_{1}-m_{0})^{2}+(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0})^{2}=1
, it implies that necessarily,
\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0}\leq 1\iff\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{0}}\leq\frac{1}{\sigma_{0}}+1
. Thus, we find that the biggest possible
\alpha\geq 1
satisfying the inequality (
12.227
) is
\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0}}
as

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}\geq\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{0}}\iff\alpha\frac{\sigma_{0}-\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{0}}\geq-\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{0}}\iff\alpha\leq\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0}},

(12.228)

and for
\alpha=\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0}}
,
\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}=\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{0}}
. In this case,
1-\alpha=-\frac{\sigma_{0}}{\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0}}
and hence the geodesic ray can be extended at least to
[-\frac{\sigma_{0}}{\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{0}},+\infty[
.
∎

12.8
Appendix of
Chapter

10

12.8.1
Proofs of
Section

10.3

Proof of
Proposition

10.1

Proof of
Proposition

10.1
.

Let
\gamma\in\Pi_{E,F}(\mu,\nu)
, then:

\displaystyle\iint L(x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)\mathrm{d}\gamma(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

(12.229)

\displaystyle=\iint\Big(\iint L(x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})\gamma_{E^{\bot}\times F^{\bot}|E\times F}\big((x_{E},y_{F}),(\mathrm{d}x_{E^{\bot}},\mathrm{d}y_{F^{\bot}})\big)

\displaystyle\gamma_{E^{\bot}\times F^{\bot}|E\times F}\big((x_{E}^{\prime},y_{F}^{\prime}),(\mathrm{d}x_{E^{\bot}}^{\prime},\mathrm{d}y_{F^{\bot}}^{\prime})\big)\Big)\mathrm{d}\gamma_{E\times F}^{*}(x_{E},y_{F})\mathrm{d}\gamma_{E\times F}^{*}(x_{E}^{\prime},y_{F}^{\prime}).

However, for
\gamma_{E\times F}^{*}
a.e.
(x_{E},y_{F}),(x_{E}^{\prime},y_{F}^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle\iint L(x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})\gamma_{E^{\bot}\times F^{\bot}|E\times F}\big((x_{E},y_{F}),(\mathrm{d}x_{E^{\bot}},\mathrm{d}y_{F^{\bot}})\big)\gamma_{E^{\bot}\times F^{\bot}|E\times F}\big((x_{E}^{\prime},y_{F}^{\prime}),(\mathrm{d}x_{E^{\bot}}^{\prime},\mathrm{d}y_{F^{\bot}}^{\prime})\big)

(12.230)

\displaystyle\geq\iint L(x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})\gamma_{E^{\bot}\times F^{\bot}|E\times F}^{*}\big((x_{E},y_{F}),(\mathrm{d}x_{E^{\bot}},\mathrm{d}y_{F^{\bot}})\big)\gamma_{E^{\bot}\times F^{\bot}|E\times F}^{*}\big((x_{E}^{\prime},y_{F}^{\prime}),(\mathrm{d}x_{E^{\bot}}^{\prime},\mathrm{d}y_{F^{\bot}}^{\prime})\big)

by definition of the Monge–Knothe coupling.
By integrating with respect to
\gamma_{E\times F}^{*}
, we obtain:

\iint L(x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)\mathrm{d}\gamma(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\geq\iint L(x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\pi_{\mathrm{MK}}(x,y)\mathrm{d}\pi_{\mathrm{MK}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}).

(12.231)

Therefore,
\pi_{\mathrm{MK}}
is optimal for subspace optimal plans.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

10.2

Proof of
Proposition

10.2
.

We first deal with
L(x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})=\big(\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}-\|y-y^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{2}
. Let
f_{E^{\bot}}
be an isometry
w.r.t

c(x_{E^{\bot}},x^{\prime}_{E^{\bot}})=\|x_{E^{\bot}}-x^{\prime}_{E^{\bot}}\|_{2}^{2}
, and let
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
be defined such as for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
f(x)=(x_{E},f_{E^{\bot}}(x_{E^{\bot}}))
.

From
Lemma

12.1
, we know that
\Pi(f_{\#}\mu,\nu)=\{(f,Id)_{\#}\gamma|\ \gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)\}
. We can rewrite:

\displaystyle\Pi_{E,F}(f_{\#}\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\{\gamma\in\Pi(f_{\#}\mu,\nu)|(\pi^{E},\pi^{F})_{\#}\gamma=\gamma_{E\times F}^{*}\}

(12.232)

\displaystyle=\{(f,Id)_{\#}\gamma|\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu),(\pi^{E},\pi^{F})_{\#}(f,Id)_{\#}\gamma=\gamma_{E\times F}^{*}\}

\displaystyle=\{(f,Id)_{\#}\gamma|\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu),(\pi^{E},\pi^{F})_{\#}\gamma=\gamma_{E\times F}^{*}\}

\displaystyle=\{(f,Id)_{\#}\gamma|\gamma\in\Pi_{E,F}(\mu,\nu)\}

using
f=(Id_{E},f_{E^{\bot}})
,
\pi^{E}\circ f=Id_{E}
and
(\pi^{E},\pi^{F})_{\#}(f,Id)_{\#}\gamma=(\pi^{E},\pi^{F})_{\#}\gamma
.

Now, for all
\gamma\in\Pi_{E,F}(f_{\#}\mu,\nu)
, there exists
\tilde{\gamma}\in\Pi_{E,F}(\mu,\nu)
such that
\gamma=(f,Id)_{\#}\tilde{\gamma}
, and we can disintegrate
\tilde{\gamma}
with respect to
\gamma_{E\times F}^{*}
:

\tilde{\gamma}=\gamma_{E\times F}^{*}\otimes K

(12.233)

with
K
a probability kernel on
(E\times F,\mathcal{B}(E^{\bot})\otimes\mathcal{B}(F^{\bot}))
.

For
\gamma_{E\times F}^{*}
almost every
(x_{E},y_{F}),\ (x_{E}^{\prime},y_{F}^{\prime})
, we have:

\displaystyle\iint\big(\|x_{E}-x_{E}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}+\|x_{E^{\bot}}-x_{E^{\bot}}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}-\|y_{F}-y_{F}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}-\|y_{F^{\bot}}-y_{F^{\bot}}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{2}

(12.234)

\displaystyle(f_{E^{\bot}},Id)_{\#}K((x_{E},y_{F}),(\mathrm{d}x_{E^{\bot}},\mathrm{d}y_{F^{\bot}}))(f_{E^{\bot}},Id)_{\#}K((x_{E}^{\prime},y_{F}^{\prime}),(\mathrm{d}x_{E^{\bot}}^{\prime},\mathrm{d}y_{F^{\bot}}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\iint\big(\|x_{E}-x_{E}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}+\|f_{E^{\bot}}(x_{E^{\bot}})-f_{E^{\bot}}(x_{E^{\bot}}^{\prime})\|_{2}^{2}-\|y_{F}-y_{F}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}-\|y_{F^{\bot}}-y_{F^{\bot}}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle K((x_{E},y_{F}),(\mathrm{d}x_{E^{\bot}},\mathrm{d}y_{F^{\bot}}))K((x_{E}^{\prime},y_{F}^{\prime}),(\mathrm{d}x_{E^{\bot}}^{\prime},\mathrm{d}y_{F^{\bot}}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\iint\big(\|x_{E}-x_{E}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}+\|x_{E^{\bot}}-x_{E^{\bot}}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}-\|y_{F}-y_{F}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}-\|y_{F^{\bot}}-y_{F^{\bot}}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle K((x_{E},y_{F}),(\mathrm{d}x_{E^{\bot}},\mathrm{d}y_{F^{\bot}}))K((x_{E}^{\prime},y_{F}^{\prime}),(\mathrm{d}x_{E^{\bot}}^{\prime},\mathrm{d}y_{F^{\bot}}^{\prime}))

using in the last line that
\|f_{E^{\bot}}(x_{E^{\bot}})-f_{E^{\bot}}(x_{E^{\bot}}^{\prime})\|_{2}=\|x_{E^{\bot}}-x_{E^{\bot}}^{\prime}\|_{2}
since
f_{E^{\bot}}
is an isometry.

By integrating with respect to
\gamma_{E\times F}^{*}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\iint\Big(\iint\big(\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}-\|y-y^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\big)^{2}

(12.235)
